|\partial^{\beta}_{x}w_{\nu}(x,y)|\leq C\exp(Cr),\,\,|\beta|,\nu\leq N_{0},\quad r=d_{\tilde{g}}(x,y),

for some uniform constant
C
(depending on

\tilde{g}
and
N_{0}
). We also have the similar bound for the distance function

(2.80)

|\partial^{\beta}_{x,y}d_{\tilde{g}}(x,y)|\leq C\exp(Cr),\,\,|\beta|\leq N_{0},\quad r=d_{\tilde{g}}(x,y).

The facts that we have just recited are well known.
One can see, for instance,
[
3
]
or
[
42
, §1.1, §3.6]
for
background regarding the Hadamard parametrix,
and
[
44
]
for a discussion
of properties of
w_{0}
.

Let us focus on the
\nu=0
term. The higher order terms can be treated similarly and satisfy better bounds, and the error term involving
R_{N}
certainly satisfies desired bound by using (
2.63
),(
2.77
), and an integration by parts argument in the
t
variable. The kernel of the main term in (
2.103
) is

(2.81)

\displaystyle K(x,y)=(2\pi)^{-2n}\lambda^{2n}

\displaystyle\iiiint\beta(2^{-j}t)e^{it\lambda-t\delta}e^{i\lambda\langle x-z,\xi\rangle}\psi_{k}(x)(1-a_{k}(x,\xi))\beta((p(x,\xi))

\displaystyle\cdot\tilde{\psi}_{k}(z)\tilde{\chi}_{0}(y)\cdot w_{0}(z,y)e^{i\lambda d_{\tilde{g}}(z,y)\eta_{1}}\cos(t\lambda|\eta|)dzd\xi d\eta dt.

We can replace
\cos(t\lambda|\eta|)
by
e^{-it\lambda|\eta|}
since the term involving

e^{it\lambda|\eta|}
is rapidly decreasing through integration by part in the
t
-variable. A similar integration by parts argument in the
z,\eta
variables also shows that we may assume
{\eta_{1}}/{p(x,\xi)}\in[1-\delta_{2},1+\delta_{2}]
for some sufficiently small
\delta_{2}
.

We claim that we have for
z\in\mathrm{supp}\,\tilde{\psi}_{k}

and
y\in\mathrm{supp}\,\tilde{\chi}_{0}
,

(2.82)

|\nabla_{z}d_{\tilde{g}}(z,y)-\omega_{k}|\leq{\frac{\delta_{1}}{10}}.

This is because

\Phi_{-t}(z,\nabla_{z}d_{\tilde{g}}(z,y))=(y,\xi_{0})

for some
\xi_{0}
and
t=d_{\tilde{g}}(z,y)
, which by (
2.69
) implies
y\notin\mathrm{supp}\,\tilde{\chi}_{0}
if
\left|{\nabla_{z}d_{\tilde{g}}(z,y)}-\omega_{k}\right|>\delta_{1}/10
.

Recall that
{\eta_{1}}/{p(x,\xi)}\in[1-\delta_{2},1+\delta_{2}]
. By choosing
\delta_{2}
small enough, (
2.82
) implies that
for any
(x,\xi)\in\mathrm{supp}\,a_{k,1}(x,\xi)
, we have

|\eta_{1}\nabla_{z}d_{\tilde{g}}(z,y)-\xi|\geq{\frac{\delta_{1}}{2}}.

Hence a simple
integration by parts argument in the
z

variable yields that
the kernel in (
2.81
) is
O(\lambda^{-N})
, which completes the proof.

Now we give the proof of (
2.65
). By (
2.64
) and our previous results for the operators
T_{j}\tilde{\chi}_{0}
when
2^{j}\leq C_{0}
and
2^{j}\geq c_{0}\log\lambda
, proving (
2.65
) is equivalent to showing that

(2.83)

\|\sum_{k}A_{k,0}(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}+(\lambda+i\delta)^{2})^{-1}(\tilde{\chi}_{0}h)\|_{L^{q}(S)}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1}\|h\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{M})}.

To prove (
2.83
), it suffices to show

(2.84)

\|\sum_{k}A_{k,0}(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}+(\lambda-i\delta)^{2})^{-1}(\tilde{\chi}_{0}h)\|_{L^{q}(S)}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1}\|h\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{M})},

as well as

(2.85)

\|\sum_{k}A_{k,0}\big((\Delta_{\tilde{g}}+(\lambda+i\delta)^{2})^{-1}-(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}+(\lambda-i\delta)^{2})^{-1}\big)(\tilde{\chi}_{0}h)\|_{L^{q}(S)}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1}\|h\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{M})}.

Note that if we define

E_{\lambda,k}={\bf 1}_{[\lambda+k\delta,\lambda+(k+1)\delta)}(\tilde{P})
, then
the symbol of the operator

E_{\lambda,k}\big((\Delta_{\tilde{g}}+(\lambda+i\delta)^{2})^{-1}-(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}+(\lambda-i\delta)^{2})^{-1}\big)

is
O\left((\lambda\delta)^{-1}(1+|k|)^{-2}\right)
. Thus (
2.85
) can be proved using the same arguments as in the proof of (
2.53
).

To prove (
2.84
), note that by taking the complex conjugate of both side of (
2.37
), we have

(2.86)

\left(\Delta_{\tilde{g}}+(\lambda-i\delta)^{2}\right)^{-1}=\frac{i}{(\lambda-i\delta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-it\lambda-t\delta}\cos(t\tilde{P})\,dt.

As in (
2.38
), if we define

(2.87)

\bar{T}_{j}f=\frac{i}{(\lambda-i\delta)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta(2^{-j}t)e^{-it\lambda-t\delta}\cos(t\tilde{P})f\,dt,

then the above arguments implies that the analog of (
2.84
), involving the operators
\bar{T}_{j}\tilde{\chi}_{0}
for
2^{j}\leq C_{0}
and
2^{j}\geq c_{0}\log\lambda
, satisfies the desired bound. Thus, it suffices to show

(2.88)

\|\sum_{\{j:C_{0}\leq 2^{j}\leq c_{0}\log\lambda\}}\sum_{k}A_{k,0}\bar{T}_{j}(\tilde{\chi}_{0}h)\|_{L^{q}(S)}\lesssim_{N}\lambda^{-N}\|h\|_{L^{2}(\tilde{M})}.

By applying the Hadamard parametrix and using (
2.70
), the proof of (
2.88
) follows similarly to that of (
2.66
). Hence, we omit the details here.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

As in the proof of Proposition
2.3
, it suffices to show the following equivalent version of (
2.32
),

(2.89)

\left\|\chi_{1}\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda+i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}\tilde{\chi}_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}\to L^{q}}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1}.

And if we fix
\beta\in C^{\infty}_{0}((1/4,4))
with
\beta=1
in (1/2,2), it suffices to show

(2.90)

\left\|\chi_{1}\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda+i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}\beta(P/\lambda)\tilde{\chi}_{1}\right\|_{L^{2}\to L^{q}}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1},

since the analogous estimate involving
(I-\beta(P/\lambda))

follows from Sobolev estimates and does not require the
\chi_{1}
and
\tilde{\chi}_{1}
cutoff functions.

We shall need the following lemma which is analogous to Lemma
2.8
:

Lemma 2.9
.

There exist zero-order pseudodifferential operators
A_{\pm}
with compactly supported Schwartz kernel such that

(2.91)

\beta(P/\lambda)\tilde{\chi}_{1}=A_{+}+A_{-}+R,

with

\|R\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}.

In local coordinates,
A_{\pm}
is of the form

(2.92)

A_{\pm}u(x)=(2\pi)^{-n}\lambda^{n}\int\int e^{i\lambda\langle x-y,\xi\rangle}A_{\pm}(x,y,\xi)u(y)dyd\xi,\quad A_{\pm}(x,y,\xi)\in C_{0}^{\infty}(T^{*}M).

For all
(x,y,\xi)\in\mathrm{supp}\,A_{+}(x,y,\xi)
, if
(x(t),\xi(t))=\Phi_{t}(x,\xi)
, we have

(2.93)

{{\rm dist}}(x(t),\mathrm{supp}\,\chi_{1})\geq 1\,\,\,\text{for}\,\,\,t\geq C,

for some sufficiently large constant C. Similarly, for all
(x,y,\xi)\in\mathrm{supp}\,A_{-}(x,y,\xi)
, we have

(2.94)

{{\rm dist}}(x(t),\mathrm{supp}\,\chi_{1})\geq 1\,\,\,\text{as}\,\,\,t\leq-C.

Proof.

As in (
2.58
), if we extend
\beta
to be an even function, then we can write
\beta(P/\lambda)=B+C
where
\|C\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\lesssim_{N}\lambda^{-N}
, and
B
is a pseudodifferential operator with principal symbol
\beta(p(x,\xi))
,
with
p(x,\xi)
here now being the principal symbol of
P
.

Next, choose
\psi\in C_{0}^{\infty}{(M)}
with
\psi=1
in a neighborhood of the support of
\tilde{\chi}_{1}
and
\psi=0
on
M_{tr}
. Without loss of generality, we may assume both
\psi
and
\tilde{\chi}_{1}
are supported in a sufficiently small neighborhood of some fixed point
y_{0}
. Then, in normal coordinates around
y_{0}
, if
B(x,y)
is the Schwartz kernel of
B
,
we have
B(x,y)\tilde{\chi}_{1}(y)=\psi(x)B(x,y)\tilde{\chi}_{1}(y)+O(\lambda^{-N})
. Since
B
has principal symbol
\beta(p(x,\xi))
,

(2.95)

B(x,y)\tilde{\chi}_{1}(y)=(2\pi)^{-n}\lambda^{n}\int e^{i\lambda\langle x-y,\xi\rangle}\psi(x){\beta(p(x,\xi))}\tilde{\chi}_{1}(y)d\xi+R(x,y),

where
R
is a lower order pseudodifferential operator which satisfies
\|R\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}=O(\lambda^{-1})
.

Let
S=\{(x,\xi)\in S^{*}M:x\in\mathrm{supp}\,\psi(x)\}
.
Since
\psi(x)=0
on
M_{tr}
and
\Gamma_{+}\cap\Gamma_{-}\subset M_{tr}
, the two sets
\Gamma_{+}\cap S
and
\Gamma_{-}\cap S
are disjoint. Since
\Gamma_{\pm}
are closed and
S
is compact,
there exists
\phi_{\pm}\in C_{0}^{\infty}(S^{*}M)
subordinate to the open cover
S\subset(U\setminus\Gamma_{-})\cup(U\setminus\Gamma_{+})
where
U
is a small neighbourhood of
S
, such that

(2.96)

\phi_{+}(x,\xi)+\phi_{-}(x,\xi)=1,\quad(x,\xi)\in S,

with
\mathrm{supp}\,\phi_{+}\cap\Gamma_{-}=\emptyset

and
\mathrm{supp}\,\phi_{-}\cap\Gamma_{+}=\emptyset
. If we define the operators
A_{\pm}
by

(2.97)

A_{\pm}f(x)=(2\pi)^{-n}\lambda^{n}\int e^{i\lambda\langle x-y,\xi\rangle}\phi_{\pm}(x,\xi/|\xi|_{g})\psi(x)\beta(p(x,\xi))\tilde{\chi}_{1}(y)f(y)d\xi dy,

then we obtain (
2.91
). Moreover, by (
2.1
),
(x,\xi)\in\mathrm{supp}\,\phi_{\pm}
satisfies (
2.93
) and (
2.94
) for sufficiently large
C
, respectively.

∎

For later use, it is straightforward to check that the
A_{\pm}
operators satisfy

(2.98)

\|A_{\pm}\|_{L^{p}\to L^{p}}=O(1),\,\,\,\forall\,\,\,1\leq p\leq\infty.

To prove (
2.90
) it suffices to show

(2.99)

\left\|\chi_{1}\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda+i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}\circ A\right\|_{L^{2}\to L^{q}}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1},\quad A=A_{+},A_{-}.

The other term involving
R
is more straightforward to handle and does not require the
\chi_{1}
cut-off function on the left. More explicitly, by (
2.30
) along with the fact that
\|R\|_{L^{2}\to L^{2}}\lesssim\lambda^{-1}
, we have

(2.100)

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda+i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}R\,(h)\right\|_{L^{q}(M)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1}(\log\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|R(h)\|_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-2}(\log\lambda)^{\frac{1}{2}}\|h\|_{L^{2}(M)},

which is better than the required estimate in (
2.90
).

Let us first prove (
2.99
) for
A=A_{+}
. The main strategy in the proof
is similar to what was used in the proof of Proposition
2.3
. If we define
T_{j}
as in (
2.38
), it is natural to separately consider
the contribution of the terms with

2^{j}\lesssim 1
,
1\lesssim 2^{j}\lesssim\log\lambda
and
\log\lambda\lesssim 2^{j}
.

(i)
2^{j}\leq 10C
for
C
as in (
2.94
).

One can directly apply the arguments from case (i) in the proof of Proposition
2.3
to handle this case. There is no need to make use of
\chi_{1}
and
A
operator here.

(ii)
2^{j}\geq c_{0}\log\lambda
for some small enough
c_{0}
.

Let

E_{\lambda,k}={\bf 1}_{[\lambda+k/\log\lambda,\lambda+(k+1)/\log\lambda)}(P).

Then by integration by parts in
t
-variable, the symbol of

S_{k}=\frac{1}{i(\lambda+i(\log\lambda)^{-1})}E_{\lambda,k}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{it\lambda-t/\log\lambda}\cos(tP)\,\sum_{2^{j}\geq c_{0}\log\lambda}\beta(2^{-j}t)\,dt

is
O\left(\lambda^{-1}\log\lambda(1+|k|)^{-N}\right)
. Thus, by the sharp spectral projection bound in (
1.13
) and (
2.35
) with
\delta=(\log\lambda)^{-1}

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle{\bf 1}_{[\lambda/2,2\lambda]}(P)\,\sum_{|k|\lesssim\lambda\log\lambda}(S_{k}\circ Ah)\|_{L^{q}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{|k|\lesssim\lambda\log\lambda}\|{\bf 1}_{[\lambda/2,2\lambda]}(P)\,(S_{k}\circ Ah)\|_{L^{q}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{\mu(q)}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\sum_{|k|\lesssim\lambda\log\lambda}\|{\bf 1}_{[\lambda/2,2\lambda]}(P)\,(S_{k}\circ Ah)\|_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\sum_{|k|\lesssim\lambda\log\lambda}(1+|k|)^{-N}\lambda^{-1}\log\lambda\|{\bf 1}_{[\lambda/2,2\lambda]}(P)E_{\lambda,k}\circ(Ah)\|_{L^{2}(M)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1}\|h\|_{L^{2}(M)},

using (
2.35
), (
2.97
) and (
2.98
) in the last step.
The case when the spectrum is outside
[\lambda/2,2\lambda]
can be handled using Sobolev estimates and satisfies better bounds.

(iii)
10C\leq 2^{j}\leq c_{0}\log\lambda
for
C
as in (
2.94
).

This is the case where we require compact cutoff functions on both sides.
By duality, it suffices to show that
the operator

(2.101)

T=\sum_{\{j:10C\leq 2^{j}\leq c_{0}\log\lambda\}}\frac{i}{(\lambda-i(\log\lambda)^{-1})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta(2^{-j}t)e^{-it\lambda-t/\log\lambda}A\circ\cos(tP)\chi_{1}\,dt

satisfies the same
L^{q^{\prime}}\to L^{2}
mapping bound as in (
2.99
).

To proceed, since
(M,g)
has nonpositive sectional curvature, we can use the
Cartan-Hadamard theorem to lift the calculation up to the universal
cover of
(M,g)
using the formula
(see e.g.,
[
42
, (3.6.4)]
)

(2.102)

(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{g}})(x,y)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}})(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y})).

Here
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
is the universal cover of

(M,g)
, with
\tilde{g}
now being the Riemannian metric
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
obtained by pulling back the metric
g

via the covering map. Also,
\Gamma:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
are the
deck transformations, and
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}\in D
with
D\simeq M
being a Dirichlet fundamental domain.

Note that by finite propagation speed,
\cos(t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}})(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))=0
if
d_{g}(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))>|t|
.
Thus, for each fixed
\tilde{x}
, by using a simple volume counting argument using the fact that the injectivity radius is positive along with the bounded geometry of
(M,g)
, the number of deck transformations
\alpha
such that
d_{g}(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\lesssim c_{0}\log\lambda
is
O(\lambda^{C_{M}c_{0}})
. The proof of this, along with additional properties of manifolds with bounded geometry, will be provided in the next section (see (
3.1
)–(
3.3
) and the discussion following (
3.161
)).

Therefore, it suffices to show that for each fixed
\alpha
, we have

(2.103)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta(2^{-j}t)e^{-it\lambda-t/\log\lambda}(A\circ\cos(t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}))(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\chi_{1}(\tilde{y})\,dt\lesssim_{N}\lambda^{-N}.

Here, we slightly abuse the notation by identifying
\chi_{1}
with a compactly supported function on the fundamental domain
D
, and
A
here denotes the lift of the operator on
(M,g)

to
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
via the covering map. By applying the Hadamard parametrix and using (
2.93
), (
2.103
) follows from the same arguments as in the proof of (
2.73
). We omit the details here.

This finishes the proof of (
2.99
) if
A=A_{+}
.

Similarly, if
A=A_{-}
, we can use the arguments in the proof of Proposition
2.3
to show that

(2.104)

\left\|\chi_{1}\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda-i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}\circ A\right\|_{L^{2}\to L^{q}}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1},

as well as

(2.105)

\left\|\chi_{1}\left(\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda+i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}-\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda-i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}\right)\circ A\right\|_{L^{2}\to L^{q}}\\
\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q)-1}.

These two inequalities yield (
2.99
) with
A=A_{-}
.

By repeating the above arguments, (
2.104
) is a consequence of

(2.106)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\beta(2^{-j}t)e^{it\lambda-t/\log\lambda}(A\circ\cos(t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}))(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\chi_{1}(\tilde{y})\,dt\lesssim_{N}\lambda^{-N}.

This follows from the same arguments as in the proof of (
2.73
), utilizing Hadamard parametrix and (
2.94
).

On the other hand,
if we define

E_{\lambda,k}={\bf 1}_{[\lambda+k/\log\lambda,\lambda+(k+1)/\log\lambda)}(P)
, then
the symbol of the operator

E_{\lambda,k}\left(\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda+i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}-\left(\Delta_{g}+(\lambda-i(\log\lambda)^{-1})^{2}\right)^{-1}\right)

is
O\left(\lambda^{-1}\log\lambda(1+|k|)^{-2}\right)
. Thus, (
2.105
) can be proved using the same arguments as in case (ii) of the proof of Proposition
2.4
.
∎

3.
Manifolds of bounded geometry

As we mentioned before, using the assumption of bounded geometry, we shall be able to modify the arguments that were
used in
[
4
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
33
]
and
[
32
]
to obtain (
1.9
), (
1.12
) and (
1.13
) in the special
case where
(M,g)
was a
compact
Riemannian manifold. The basic facts that will allow us to carry out the
local harmonic analysis for general manifolds of bounded geometry can be found, for instance, in
Chapter 2 of Eldering
[
26
]
. In addition to extending the local harmonic analysis that was used in these
earlier works, we shall also need to show that the global kernel estimates in
[
6
]
and the aforementioned earlier
works hold for manifolds of bounded geometry and nonpositive curvature. As we shall see in the end of the section,
like in the earlier works, we can do this by lifting the calculations up to the universal cover and exploiting the fact that

r_{\rm{Inj}}(M)>0
if
M
is of bounded geometry. After possibly multiplying the metric by a constant, we may also assume
that
r_{\rm{Inj}}(M)>1
, as we shall do throughout this section.

Let us quickly review facts that we shall require for our arguments. First, there is a
\delta=\delta(M)>0
so that the coordinate
charts given by the exponential map are defined on all geodesic balls
B(x,2\delta)
,
x\in M
, of radius
2\delta>0
.
Furthermore, in the resulting normal coordinates, the Riemannian distance,
d_{g}(x_{1},x_{2})
, is comparable to

|\exp^{-1}_{x}(x_{1})-\exp^{-1}_{x}(x_{2})|
, independent of
x\in M
.
Additionally, derivatives of the transition maps from these coordinates are also uniformly bounded.
(See Proposition 2.5 and Lemma 2.6 in
[
26
]
.)

Furthermore, there is a uniformly locally finite cover by geodesic balls.
By this we mean that there is a
\delta(M)>0
so that whenever
\delta\in(0,\delta(M)]
there is a countable
covering by geodesic balls

(3.1)

M=\bigcup_{j}B(x_{j},\delta)\quad\text{with }d_{g}(x_{j},x_{k})\geq\delta\,\,\text{if }\,j\neq k.

Furthermore, assuming that
\delta(M)
is small enough, for
\delta
as above, we can
assume that the covering also satisfies

(3.2)

\text{Card}\bigl\{j:\,B(x_{j},2\delta)\cap B(x,2\delta)\neq\emptyset\bigr\}\leq C_{0},\,\,\,\forall\,\,x\in M,

for a uniform constant
C_{0}=C_{0}(M)<\infty
.
(See
[
8
, Lemma 2.16]
.)

From this one also sees that we can
also choose
\delta=\delta(M)>0
small enough so that there is a
C^{\infty}
partition of unity
\{\psi_{i}\}
,

(3.3)

1=\sum_{j}\psi_{j}(x),\quad\mathrm{supp}\psi_{j}\subset B(x_{j},\delta),

with uniform control of each derivative all of the
\{\psi_{j}\}
in the normal coordinates described above.
(See Lemma 2.17 and
Definition 2.9 in
[
26
]
.)

Using our assumption of bounded geometry, as we shall describe shortly, we can also construct a microlocal partition of unity involving
pseudodifferential operators supported in the
\delta
-balls in the above covering. It will be convenient, as in
the compact manifold case, to use such microlocal cutoffs for the local harmonic analysis that we shall
require in the proof of Theorems
1.3
and
1.5
. These operators will, in effect, give us a
second microlocalization needed to apply bilinear harmonic analysis.

3.1. Log-scale spectral projection estimates

Let us first show how we can adapt the proofs for the compact manifold case treated
in
[
33
]
to prove Theorem
1.5
since the second microlocalization and
the resulting arguments is a bit more straightforward than what is needed for the
Strichartz estimates in Theorem
1.3
.

Before describing these pseudodifferential cutoffs, let us introduce another local operator which we shall require. To do so, let us
fix, following
[
33
]
,
\rho\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
satisfying

(3.4)

\rho(0)=1,\quad\hat{\rho}(t)=0,\,\,t\notin\delta_{1}\cdot[1-\delta_{2},1+\delta_{2}]=[\delta_{1}-\delta_{1}\delta_{2},\,\delta_{1}+\delta_{1}\delta_{2}],\\
\text{with }\,0<\delta_{1},\delta_{2}<\tfrac{1}{2}\min(r_{\rm{Inj}}(M),1)

as above, with
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}

to be specified later when in order to apply
bilinear oscillatory integral results from
[
38
]
and
[
47
]
, just as was done
in
[
33
]
. We then define the “local” operators

(3.5)

\sigma_{\lambda}=\rho(\lambda-P)+\rho(\lambda+P).

We call these local since their kernels satisfy

(3.6)

\sigma_{\lambda}(x,y)=0\quad\text{if }\,\,d_{g}(x,y)>r,\,\,r=\delta_{1}(1+\delta_{2})<\delta/2.

This follows from the fact that, since
\hat{\rho}
has support as in (
3.4
) we have, by Euler’s formula,

(3.7)

\sigma_{\lambda}=\pi^{-1}\int_{0}^{\delta_{1}(1+\delta_{2})}\hat{\rho}(t)\,e^{i\lambda t}\,\cos(tP)\,dt.

Finally, by finite propagation speed,
(\cos(tP))(x,y)=0
if
d_{g}(x,y)>|t|
, which, along with the preceding
identity, yields (
3.6
).

We also note that
by (
1.4
) and orthogonality we have

(3.8)

\|\sigma_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q}=O(\lambda^{\mu(q)}),

with
\mu(q)
as in (
1.3
).
We shall also consider the “global” smoothed out spectral projection operators

(3.9)

\rho_{\lambda}=\rho(T(\lambda-P)),\quad T=c_{0}\log\lambda,

where
c_{0}>0
shall be fixed later. We then conclude that, in order to prove
Theorem
1.5
it suffices to show that if all of the sectional curvatures
of
M
are nonpositive then we have

(3.10)

\|\rho_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q}\lesssim\begin{cases}\lambda^{\mu(q)}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2},\quad\text{if }\,\,q>q_{c}\\
\bigl(\lambda(\log\lambda)^{-1}\bigr)^{\mu(q)},\quad\text{if }\,\,q\in(2,q_{c}],\end{cases}

while, if all the sectional curvatures are all pinched below
-\kappa^{2}_{0}
with
\kappa_{0}>0
, we have the stronger estimates

(3.11)

\|\rho_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q}\leq C_{q}\,\lambda^{\mu(q)}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2},\,\,q\in(2,\infty].

For later use, note that by (
1.4
) and a simple orthogonality argument we
have

(3.12)

\|(I-\sigma_{\lambda})\circ\rho_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q}\leq C\lambda^{\mu(q)}\,(\log\lambda)^{-1},\,\,q\in(2,\infty].

As in earlier works, the task of obtaining the bounds in (
3.10
) and (
3.11
)
naturally splits into three cases:
q=q_{c}
,
q\in(q_{c},\infty]
and
q\in(2,q_{c})
. Handling
the critical exponent is the most difficult. So, we shall first prove the special case
of these to bounds for this exponent:

(3.13)

\|\rho_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q_{c}}\lesssim\bigl(\lambda(\log\lambda)^{-1}\bigr)^{\mu(q_{c})},\\
\text{if all of the sectional curvatures of }\,M\,\,\text{are nonpositive},

and the stronger results

(3.14)

\|\rho_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q_{c}}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q_{c})}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2},\\
\text{if all of the sectional curvatures of }\,M\,\,\text{are }\leq-\kappa_{0}^{2},\,\,\,\text{some }\,\kappa_{0}>0.

Let us now describe the microlocal operators which will be utilized to give us our
very useful second microlocalization.
We shall use the fact that for each fixed
j
, we can write

(3.15)

\psi_{j}(x)(\sigma_{\lambda}h)(x)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\bigl(A_{j,\ell}\circ\sigma_{\lambda}\bigr)(h)(x)+R_{j}h(x),

where the
A_{j,l}
are pseudodifferential operators in a bounded subset of
S^{0}_{1,0}

and the kernels of the above operators satisfy

(3.16)

A_{j,\ell}(x,y),\,\,R_{j}(x,y)=0,\quad\text{if }\,x\notin B(x_{j},\delta)\,\,\text{or }\,\,y\notin B(x_{j},3\delta/2).

Furthermore, in the normal coordinate system about
x_{j}
described above
we may assume that

(3.17)

A_{j,\ell}(x,y)=(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n}}e^{i\langle x-y,\xi\rangle}a^{\lambda}_{j,\ell}(x,y,\xi)\,d\xi

with
a^{\lambda}_{j,\ell}
supported in an
O(K^{-1/(n-1)})
conic neighborhood of some

\xi_{j,\ell}\in S^{n-1}
,
a^{\lambda}_{j,\ell}(\xi)=0
if
|\xi|\notin[c_{1}\lambda,\lambda/c_{1}]
, with

c_{1}\in(0,1)
independent of
j
. So, if
K
is large enough, we may assume that

(3.18)

a^{\lambda}_{j,\ell}(x,y,\xi)=0\,\,\text{when }\,x\notin B(x_{j},\delta),\,y\notin B(x_{j},2\delta),\,\,\text{or }\,\bigl|\tfrac{\xi_{j,\ell}}{|\xi_{j,\ell|}}-\tfrac{\xi}{|\xi|}\bigr|\geq\delta,

and also that this symbol satisfies the natural size estimates corresponding to
these support properties

\partial^{\alpha_{1}}_{x,y}\partial^{\alpha_{2}}_{\xi}a^{\lambda}_{j,\ell}=O_{\delta}(\lambda^{-|\alpha_{2}|}).

In addition to (
3.16
), we may assume by fixing
c_{1}>0
small enough that we have the uniform
bounds

(3.19)

R_{j}(x,y)=O(\lambda^{-N}),\quad N=1,2,\dots.

The above dyadic pseudodifferential operators satisfy

(3.20)

\|A_{j,\ell}\|_{L^{p}(M)\to L^{p}(M)}=O(1),\quad 1\leq p\leq\infty.

One constructs the above microlocal cutoffs
A_{j,\ell}
using standard arguments
from the theory of pseudodifferential operators. The resulting symbols can have
dyadic support
|\xi|\approx\lambda
, just as in the case of compact manifolds treated
in
[
33
]
, since the left side of (
3.15
) involves
\sigma_{\lambda}=\sigma(\lambda-P)
.
Furthermore, using the fact that we are assuming that
M
is of bounded geometry,
by the above discussion, the implicit constants in the above description of this
second microlocalization
can be chosen to be independent
of
j
if
\delta\leq\delta(M)
and
K
in (
3.15
)
are fixed.

We also note that, due to our assumptions,
we can assume that the symbols
a^{\lambda}_{j,\ell}
vanish
outside of a small conic neighborhood of
(x_{j},\xi_{j,\ell})

by choosing
\delta\leq\delta(M)
to be small and
K
to large. As in the
compact manifold case, this will be useful when we
need to use our local harmonic analysis.

One consequence of this, (
3.2
), (
3.16
) and (
3.20
) is that if we fix

\ell_{0}\in\{1,\dots,K\}
, then
these dyadic operators satisfy

(3.21)

A=A_{\ell_{0}}=\sum_{j}A_{j,\ell_{0}}\in S^{0}_{1,0}\quad\text{and }\,\|A\|_{L^{p}(M)\to L^{p}(M)}=O(1),\,\,1\leq p\leq\infty.

These microlocal cutoffs will play the role of the “
B
” operators
that were used for compact manifolds in
in
[
4
]
,
[
7
]
and
[
33
]
.
Note also that by (
3.2
) and (
3.16
) we also have

(3.22)

\|Rh\|_{L^{q}(M)}\leq C_{p,q,M}\,\|h\|_{L^{p}(M)},\,\,1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty,\,\,\text{if },\,\,R=\sum_{j}R_{j}.

Note that, in view of (
3.3
), (
3.12
), (
3.15
), (
3.21
) and (
3.22
), in order
to prove (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), it suffices to
prove that if all the sectional curvatures of

M
are nonpositive

(3.23)

\|A\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q_{c}}\lesssim\bigl(\lambda(\log\lambda)^{-1}\bigr)^{\mu(q_{c})},

while, if all the sectional curvatures are all pinched below
-\kappa^{2}_{0}
with
\kappa_{0}>0
, we have

(3.24)

\|A\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q_{c}}\lesssim\,\lambda^{\mu(q_{c})}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2}.

Using (
3.2
) and (
3.16
), we have for
q\geq 2

|A\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f(x)|\leq C\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)\|_{\ell^{q}_{j}}.

Consequently, if we consider the vector-valued operators

(3.25)

{\mathcal{A}}h=(A_{1,\ell_{0}}h,\,A_{2,\ell_{0}}h,\dots)

we have

(3.26)

\|A\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q}(M)}\lesssim\|{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|_{L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}(M\times{\mathbb{N}})},\quad q\in[2,\infty].

Note for later use that by (
3.2
), (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) we also have

(3.27)

\|{\mathcal{A}}h\|_{L^{p}_{x}\ell^{p}_{j}}\leq C\|h\|_{L^{p}(M)},\quad 1\leq p\leq\infty.

In view of (
3.26
), in order to prove (
3.23
) and (
3.24
), it suffices to show that when
all of the sectional curvatures of
M
are nonpositive
we have

(3.28)

\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}}\lesssim\bigl(\lambda(\log\lambda)^{-1}\bigr)^{\mu(q_{c})}\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)},

while, if all the sectional curvatures are all pinched below
-\kappa^{2}_{0}
with
\kappa_{0}>0
, we have

(3.29)

\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q_{c})}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

The operators
{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}
play the role of the
\tilde{\rho}_{\lambda}
operators in
[
7
]
and
[
33
]
.
We are introducing this vector-valued approach to easily allow us to only have to carry out the local bilinear harmonic analysis
in individual coordinate patches coming from the geodesic normal coordinates in the balls
B(x_{j},2\delta)
mentioned
before. In the compact case treated by two of us and coauthors, this was not necessary since
M
could be covered
by finitely many balls of sufficiently small radius on which the bilinear analysis could be carried out.

In proving these two estimates we may, of course, assume,
as we shall throughout this section, that

(3.30)

\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}=1.

Then, similar to the compact manifold case, let us
define vector-valued sets

(3.31)

\begin{split}A_{+}&=\{(x,j):\,|({\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x,j)|\geq\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\frac{1}{8}}\}\\
A_{-}&=\{(x,j):\,|({\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x,j)|<\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\frac{1}{8}}\}.\end{split}

Recall here that

(3.32)

({\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x,j)=A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f(x).

In order to prove (
3.28
) and (
3.29
),
it suffices to show that
we have the following two results. First, for
all complete manifolds of bounded
geometry and nonpositive
sectional curvatures, we have for
\lambda\gg 1

the large height estimates

(3.33)

\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}(A_{+})}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q_{c})}T^{-1/2},\\
\text{if }T=c_{0}\log\lambda,\,\,\text{with }c_{0}=c_{0}(M)>0\,\,\text{sufficiently small.}

The remaining estimate, for small heights,
which would yield the above
desired bounds for
q=q_{c}
then would be the following
for
T
as above

(3.34)

\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}(A_{-})}\\
\lesssim\begin{cases}\bigl(\lambda T^{-1}\bigr)^{\mu(q_{c})},\,\,\text{if all the sectional curvatures of }M\,\text{are nonpositive}\\
\lambda^{\mu(q_{c})}T^{-1/2},\,\,\text{if all the sectional curvatures of }M\,\text{are }\leq-\kappa_{0}^{2},\,\text{some }\kappa_{0}>0,\end{cases}

with
T
in (
3.9
) as in the preceding inequality.

In order to prove (
3.33
) and also the estimates
in Theorem
1.5
for
q>q_{c}
we shall require
the following lemma.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
\Psi=|\rho|^{2}
and fix

a\in C^{\infty}_{0}((-1,1))\,\,\text{satisfying }\,\,a(t)=1,\,\,|t|\leq 1/2.

Then, if
G_{\lambda}=G_{\lambda,T}
is defined by

(3.35)

G_{\lambda}=G_{\lambda}(P)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(1-a(t))\,T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\,e^{it\lambda}\,e^{-itP}\,dt,

we have for
c_{0}=c_{0}(M)>0
sufficiently small
and
\lambda\gg 1

(3.36)

\|G_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}=O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\exp(C_{M}T)),\,\,1\leq T\leq c_{0}\log\lambda,

assuming that
M
is of bounded geometry and that
all of its sectional curvatures are nonpositive.

Note that if
L_{\lambda}=L_{\lambda,T}
is given by

(3.37)

L_{\lambda}=L_{\lambda}(P)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}a(t)\,T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\,e^{it\lambda}e^{-itP}\,dt,

then

(3.38)

G_{\lambda}+L_{\lambda}=\Psi(T(\lambda-P))=\rho_{\lambda}\rho^{*}_{\lambda}.

Furthermore, it is simple to use (
1.4
)
and a simple orthogonality argument to see
that if
q_{c}^{\prime}
is the dual exponent for
q_{c}
then

(3.39)

\|L_{\lambda}\|_{L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}(M)\to L^{q_{c}}(M)}=O(T^{-1}\lambda^{2\mu(q_{c})})=O(T^{-1}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}).

We shall postpone the proof of this lemma until the
end of this section. Let us now see how we can use it
along with the local estimate (
3.8
) to prove
the large height estimates. As two of us did for compact
manifolds, we shall rely on a variant of an argument of
Bourgain
[
11
]
, along
with
(
3.36
) and (
3.39
).

Proof of (
3.33
).

This just follows from the proof of (2.18) in
[
33
]
; however, we shall give the argument here for the sake
of completeness. We shall be assuming
here that, as in (
3.33
),
T=c_{0}\log\lambda
, with

c_{0}>0
to be specified in a moment.

We first note that, by (
3.12
), (
3.25
) and (
3.30
)

(3.40)

\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}\leq\|{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}+C\lambda^{1/q_{c}}/\log\lambda,

since, by (
1.3
),

\mu(q_{c})=1/q_{c}.

As a result, we would obtain (
3.33
) if we could show that

(3.41)

\|{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}\leq C\lambda^{1/q_{c}}(\log\lambda)^{-1/2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}.

To prove this, similar to what was done in
[
33
]
,
choose
g=g(x,j)
vanishing outside
A_{+}
so that

(3.42)

\|g\|_{L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j}(A_{+})}=1\,\,\text{and }\,\|{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}=\sum_{j}\int({\mathcal{A}}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x,j)\,\overline{{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}(x,j)\cdot g(x,j)}\,dx.

Then, similar to (3.4) in
[
33
]
,
using (
3.30
) and (
3.38
) we find that

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{A}}\rho_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\,\int_{M}f(x)\cdot\overline{\bigl(\rho_{\lambda}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\bigr)(x)}\,dx\,\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{M}\bigl|\bigl(\rho_{\lambda}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\bigr)(x)\bigr|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\int_{M}\bigl(\bigl({\mathcal{A}}\circ\Psi(T(\lambda-P))\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\bigr)({\bf 1}\cdot g)\bigr)(x,j)\cdot\overline{({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,j)}\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\int_{M}\bigl(\bigl({\mathcal{A}}\circ L_{\lambda}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\bigr)({\bf 1}\cdot g)\bigr)(x,j)\cdot\overline{({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,j)}\,dx

\displaystyle+\sum_{j}\int_{M}\bigl(\bigl({\mathcal{A}}\circ G_{\lambda}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\bigr)({\bf 1}\cdot g)\bigr)(x,j)\cdot\overline{({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,j)}\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad=I+II.

By (
3.27
), (
3.39
) and (
3.42
) and
Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle|I|

\displaystyle\leq\|({\mathcal{A}}L_{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}^{*})({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}}\cdot\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|L_{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}}\cdot 1

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{-1}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}\|{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{-1}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j}}=T^{-1}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}.

To estimate
II
, note that, by (
3.27
),

\|{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\|_{L^{1}_{x}\ell^{1}_{j}\to L^{1}_{x}},\,\|{\mathcal{A}}\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}\to L^{\infty}_{x}\ell_{j}^{\infty}}=O(1)
.
Also, if
c_{0}>0
is chosen small enough, then,
by (
3.36
) we have

\|G_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}=O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{1}{8}}).

Therefore, we have, by the above argument,

\displaystyle|II|

\displaystyle\leq\|({\mathcal{A}}G_{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}^{*})({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}\ell^{\infty}_{j}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{1}_{x}\ell_{j}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|(G_{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}^{*})({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{\infty}_{x}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{1}_{x}\ell_{j}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{1}{8}}\|{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{1}_{x}\ell^{1}_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{1}{8}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{1}_{x}\ell^{1}_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{1}{8}}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j}(A_{+})}\cdot\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\|^{2}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}}

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}+\frac{1}{8}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\|^{2}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}}.

But, by the definition of
A_{+}
in (
3.31
)

\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\|^{2}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}}\leq\bigl(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\frac{1}{8}}\bigr)^{-2}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}.

So, assuming, as we may that
\lambda\gg 1
is large,
we have

|II|\leq C\lambda^{-1/8}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{+})}.

The estimates for
I
and
II
yield (
3.33
).
∎

Let us also see how we can use Lemma
3.1
to
obtain the bounds in Theorem
1.5
for

q>q_{c}
, which extend the results for compact
manifolds of Hassell and Tacy
[
28
]
.

Proof of
q>q_{c}
bounds.

Let us now prove the estimates in (
1.12
) for

q>q_{c}
. For a given such
q
, it suffices to show
that

\|\rho_{\lambda}\|_{2\to q}\lesssim T^{-1/2}\lambda^{\mu(q)},

with
T=c_{q}\log\lambda
,
c_{q}=c_{q}(M)>0
sufficiently small.
This in turn is equivalent to showing that

(3.43)

\|\Psi(T(\lambda-P))\|_{q^{\prime}\to q}\lesssim T^{-1}\lambda^{2\mu(q)},\,\,q>q_{c},

with
\Psi=|\rho|^{2}
, as above.

If, as in (
3.38
),
\Psi(T(\lambda-P))=L_{\lambda}+G_{\lambda}
,
it is straightforward to check that (
1.4
) yields

\|L_{\lambda}\|_{q^{\prime}\to q}\lesssim T^{-1}\lambda^{2\mu(q)}.

Furthermore, by (
3.36
) and orthogonality, we have

\|G_{\lambda}\|_{2\to 2}=O(1).

If we interpolate between this estimate and (
3.36
)
we obtain for
T\lesssim c_{0}\log\lambda
as above

\|G_{\lambda}\|_{q^{\prime}\to q}=O(\lambda^{\frac{(n-1)(q-2)}{2q}}\exp(C_{M}T)),\quad q>2.

Once checks that

\tfrac{(n-1)(q-2)}{2q}<2\mu(q)=2n(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{q})-1

if

q>q_{c}=\tfrac{2(n+1)}{n-1}
. As a result, for such
an exponent, we have, for such
q
,

\|G_{\lambda}\|_{q^{\prime}\to q}=O(\lambda^{2\mu(q)-\varepsilon_{q}})
, some

\varepsilon_{q}>0
, if, as we may assume
\lambda\gg 1
and

T=c_{q}\log\lambda
with
c_{q}>0
sufficiently small.

Since this and the above bound for
L_{\lambda}
yields
(
3.43
), the proof of the spectral projection
estimates in Theorem
1.5
for
q>q_{c}
is complete.
∎

Next, we note that we would complete the proof of the spectral projection
estimates in Theorem
1.5
for
q=q_{c}
if we could prove the low height
estimates (
3.34
) which, unlike (
3.33
), differ depending on the
curvature assumptions. For this we shall need to use local bilinear harmonic
analysis which is a variable coefficient variant of that in
Tao, Vargas and Vega
[
47
]

and relies on bilinear oscillatory integral estimates of Lee
[
38
]
.
Since the microlocal cutoffs in (
3.21
) arise from
the partition of unity in (
3.15
) corresponding to the balls
\{B(x_{j},\delta)\}

whose doubles have finite overlap, we shall be able carry out this analysis
in each ball
B(x_{j},2\delta)
using geodesic normal coordinates about the center.
Since, as we pointed out earlier, our assumption of bounded geometry ensures
bounded transition maps and uniform bounds on derivatives of the metric, we shall
be able to localize to individual balls. As a result, we just need to repeat the arguments
in the earlier work of two of use
[
33
]
for compact manifolds, which also reduced
to bilinear analysis in a fixed coordinate chart.

Just as in the earlier works for compact manifolds,
[
4
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
33
]
,
to prove (
3.34
), besides (
3.15
), we shall need to use a second microlocalization, which
involves localizing in
\theta\geq\lambda^{-1/8}
neighborhoods of geodesics in a fixed coordinate chart. To describe
this, let us fix
j
in (
3.15
), as well as
\ell_{0}\in\{1,\dots,K\}
and consider the resulting
pseudodifferential cutoff,
A_{j,\ell_{0}}
, which is a summand in (
3.21
). Its symbol then satisfies
the conditions in (
3.18
). The resulting geodesic normal coordinates on
B(x_{j},2\delta)

vanish at
x_{j}
. We then have that the metric
g_{jk}
satisfies
g_{jk}(y)=\delta^{k}_{j}+O((d_{g}(x_{j},y))^{2})
. We may also
assume that
\xi_{j,\ell_{0}}=(0,\dots,0,1)
. Since we are fixing

j
and
\ell_{0}
for now, analogous
[
33
]
, let us simplify the notation a bit by letting

(3.44)

\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}=A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda},

which is analogous to (2.10) in
[
33
]
.

For dyadic
\theta\geq\lambda^{-1/8}
, the additional microlocal cutoffs that we require correspond to

\theta
-nets of geodesics,

\{\gamma_{\nu}\}
,
in
S^{*}M
passing through points
(y,\eta)
near
(0,(0,\dots,0,1))
. To define them, fix a function

q\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{2(n-1)})
supported in
\{z:\,|z_{i}|\leq 1,\,1\leq i\leq 2(n-1)\}
satisfying

(3.45)

\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{Z}}^{2(n-1)}}q(z-k)\equiv 1.

To use this, let

\varPi=\{y:\,y_{n}=0\}

be the points in
\Omega=B(x_{j},2\delta)
whose last coordinate vanishes. Also let

y^{\prime}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1})
and
\eta^{\prime}=(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n-1})
denote the first

(n-1)
coordinates of
y
and
\eta
, respectively, with
(y,\eta)\in S^{*}\Omega
.
We shall always have
\theta\in[\lambda^{-1/8},1]
, and,

\lambda^{-1/8}
, here, of course, is related to the height decomposition (
3.31
).

We then can extend the definition of our cutoffs to a neighborhood of
(0,(0,\dots,,1))
by setting for

(x,\xi)\in S^{*}\Omega
in this neighborhood

(3.46)

q^{\theta}_{k}(x,\xi)=q(\theta^{-1}(y^{\prime},\eta^{\prime})-k)\,\,\text{if }\,\,\Phi_{s}(x,\xi)=(y^{\prime},0,\eta^{\prime},\eta_{n})\,\,\text{with }\,s=d_{g}(x,\varPi).

Here
\Phi_{s}
denotes the geodesic flow in
S^{*}\Omega
and
d_{g}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
is geodesic distance.
Consequently,
q^{\theta}_{k}(x,\xi)
is constant on all geodesics
(x(s),\xi(s))\in S^{*}\Omega
with
x(0)\in\varPi
near

0
and
\xi(0)
near
(0,\dots,0,1)
. Therefore,

(3.47)

q^{\theta}_{k}(\Phi_{s}(x,\xi))=q^{\theta}_{k}(x,\xi).

We then extend the definition of the cutoffs to a conic neighborhood of
(0,(0,\dots,0,1))
in
T^{*}\Omega\backslash 0
by
setting

(3.48)

q^{\theta}_{k}(x,\xi)=q^{\theta}_{k}(x,\xi/p(x,\xi)),

with
p(x,\xi)
being the principal symbol of
P=\sqrt{-\Delta_{g}}
.

Note also that if
(y_{\nu}^{\prime},\eta^{\prime}_{\nu})=\theta k=\nu
and
\gamma_{\nu}
is the geodesic
in
S^{*}\Omega
passing through
(y^{\prime}_{\nu},0,\eta_{\nu})\in S^{*}\Omega
with

\eta_{\nu}\in S^{*}_{(y^{\prime},0)}\Omega
having
\eta_{\nu}^{\prime}
as its first
(n-1)
coordinates
and
\eta_{n}>0
then

(3.49)

q^{\theta}_{k}(x,\xi)=0\,\,\text{if }\,\text{dist }((x,\xi),\gamma_{\nu})\geq C_{0}\theta,\,\,\nu=\theta k

for a uniform constant
C_{0}
. Also,
q_{k}^{\theta}
satisfies the estimates

(3.50)

|\partial_{x}^{\sigma}\partial_{\xi}^{\gamma}q^{\theta}_{k}(x,\xi)|\lesssim\theta^{-|\sigma|-|\gamma|},\,\,(x,\xi)\in S^{*}\Omega

related to this support property.

Next, fix
\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}_{0}
supported in
|x|<3\delta/2
which equals one when

|x|\leq 5\delta/4
. Additinally, fix
\tilde{\tilde{\psi}}\in C^{\infty}_{0}
supported in

|x|<2\delta
which equals one when
|x|\leq 3\delta/2
.
Also, fix

\tilde{\beta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}((0,\infty))
so that
\tilde{\beta}(p(x,\xi)/\lambda)
equals one in a neighborhood
of the
\xi
support of
a^{\lambda}_{j,\ell_{0}}
. We then define the compound symbols
Q^{\theta}_{\nu}=Q^{\theta}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}
and associated operators by

(3.51)

Q^{\theta}_{\nu}(x,y,\xi)=\tilde{\psi}(x)\tilde{\tilde{\psi}}(y)q^{\theta}_{k}(x,\xi)\,\tilde{\beta}((x,\xi)/\lambda),\,\,\nu=\theta k\in\theta\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}^{2(n-1)},\,\,\text{and}\\
Q^{\theta}_{\nu}h(x)=(2\pi)^{-n}\iint e^{i(x-y)\cdot\xi}Q^{\theta}_{\nu}(x,y,\xi)\,h(y)\,d\xi dy.

It follows that these dyadic pseudodifferential operators belong to a bounded subset
of
S^{0}_{7/8,1/8}
due to our assumption that
\theta\in[\lambda^{-1/8},1]
. We have constructed these
operators so that for small enough
\delta_{0}>0
we have

(3.52)

Q^{\theta}_{\nu}(x,y,\xi)=Q^{\theta}_{\nu}(z,y,\eta),\,(z,\eta)=\Phi_{t}(x,\xi),\\
\text{if }\,\text{dist }((x,\xi),\,\text{supp }A_{j,\ell_{0}})\leq\delta_{0}\,\text{and }\,\,|t|\leq 2\delta_{0}.

The compound symbol involves the cutoff
\tilde{\tilde{\psi}}(y)
which equals one on a neighborhood
of the
x
-support of
A_{j,\ell_{0}}
as well as the support of
\tilde{\psi}
.
We use cutoffs in both variables
since
M
is not assumed to be compact and we want to avoid
issues at infinity. This symbol in (
3.52
) vanishes when either
x
or
y
is outside the
2\delta
-ball about
the origin in our coordinates for
\Omega
. By (
3.7
) (
3.16
) and (
3.44
), we can fix
\delta_{1}
in (
3.4
) small enough so that
we also have, analogous to (2.41) in
[
33
]
,

(3.53)

\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\nu}\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}+R,\quad R=R_{\lambda,j,\ell_{0}},\,\,\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-1/8},\,\,\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}=A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda},\\
\text{where }\,\,R(x,y)=O(\lambda^{-N}),\forall\,N\,\,\text{and }R(x,y)=0,\,\,\text{if }x\notin B(x_{j},2\delta)\,\,\text{or }y\notin B(x_{j},2\delta),

with bounds for the remainder kernel independent of
j
.

Let us now point out straightforward but useful properties of our operators. First, by (
3.16
), (
3.53
)
and the support properties of
\tilde{\psi}
,
\tilde{\tilde{\psi}}
, we have

(3.54)

\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h={\bf 1}_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}\cdot\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}\bigl({\bf 1}_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}\cdot h\bigr),\,\,Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}=Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\\
\text{and }\,\,Rh={\bf 1}_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}\cdot R\bigl({\bf 1}_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}\cdot h\bigr),\,\,R=R_{\lambda,j,\ell_{0}}.

Also, we have the uniform bounds

(3.55)

\begin{split}\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h\|_{\ell^{q}_{\nu}L^{q}(M)}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{q}(M)},\,\,2\leq q\leq\infty\\
\bigl\|\sum_{\nu^{\prime}}(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu})^{*}H(\nu^{\prime},\,\cdot\,)\|_{L^{p}(M)}\lesssim\|H\|_{\ell^{p}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{p}(M)},\,\,1\leq p\leq 2.\end{split}

The second estimate follows via duality from the first. The first one is (2.33) in Lemma 2.2 of
[
33
]
. By interpolation,
one just needs to verify that the estimate holds for the two endpoints,
p=2
and
p=\infty
. The former follows via an almost
orthogonality argument, and the latter from the fact that we have the uniform bounds

\sup_{x\in B(x_{j},2\delta)}\int_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}(x,y)|\,dy\leq C.

See
[
33
]
for more details.

Note that if we use (
3.55
) along with (
3.51
) and the finite overlap of the balls
\{B(x_{j},2\delta)\}
we obtain
for our fixed
\ell_{0}=1,\dots,K

(3.56)

\begin{split}\bigl(\sum_{j,\nu}\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}h\|_{L^{q}(M)}^{q}\bigr)^{1/q}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{q}(M)},\,\,2\leq q\leq\infty\\
\bigl\|\sum_{j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j^{\prime},\ell_{0},\nu^{\prime}})^{*}H(\nu^{\prime},j^{\prime},\,\cdot\,)\|_{L^{p}(M)}\lesssim\|H\|_{\ell^{p}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{p}(M)},\,\,1\leq p\leq 2.\end{split}

In addition to this inequality and (
3.12
) we shall require another that follows almost directly from a result
in
[
33
]
. Specifically, we require the following commutator bounds

(3.57)

\bigl\|(A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}-A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda})h\|_{L^{q}(M)}\leq C_{q}\lambda^{\mu(q)-1/4}\|h\|_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))},

with
\mu(q)
is as in (
1.3
),
assuming that
\delta
, as well as
\delta_{1}
in (
3.4
) are fixed small enough.

To see this, let
\tilde{A}_{j,\ell_{0}}
be a 0-order pseudo-differential operator with symbol
\tilde{a}_{j,\ell_{0}}^{\lambda}(x,y,\xi)
supported in
|\xi|\approx\lambda
and equals one in
the support of the symbol
a_{j,\ell_{0}}^{\lambda}(x,y,\xi)
of the
A_{j,\ell_{0}}
operator, then it is not hard to see that

(3.58)

\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}-\tilde{A}_{j,\ell_{0}}A_{j,\ell_{0}}\|_{L^{p}_{x}\to L^{p}_{x}}=O(\lambda^{-N})\,\,\forall\,N\,\,\text{if }\,\,1\leq p\leq\infty.

And by using the fact that
the kernel
\tilde{A}_{j,\ell_{0}}(x,y)
is
O(\lambda^{n}(1+\lambda|x-y|)^{-N})
and Young’s inequality, we also have

(3.59)

\|\tilde{A}_{j,\ell_{0}}\|_{L^{2}_{x}\to L^{p}_{x}}=O(\lambda^{n(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})})\,\,\,\text{if }\,\,2\leq p\leq\infty.

Thus to prove (
3.57
) it suffices to show

(3.60)

\bigl\|(A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}-A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda})h\|_{L^{2}(M)}\leq C_{q}\lambda^{-3/4}\|h\|_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}

since
n(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p})-\frac{3}{4}\leq\mu(q)-\frac{1}{4}
for
q\leq q_{c}
.
This follows from the
proof of (2.39) in
[
33
]
since, by (
3.18
),
A_{j,\ell_{0}}f
vanishes outside

B(x_{j},2\delta)
and the two operators in (
3.60
) vanish when acting on functions vanishing on

B(x_{j},2\delta)
. This, just as in
[
33
]
, allows one to prove (
3.60
),
exactly as in
[
33
]
, by just working in a coordinate chart
(
B(x_{j},2\delta)
here) and, to obtain the inequality
using (
3.47
), (
3.52
) and Egorov’s theorem related to the properties of the half wave operator
e^{itP}
in this local coordinate.

Next, as in
[
4
]
and
[
33
]
, we note that we can write for
\theta_{0}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}
as in (
3.44
)

(3.61)

\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}h\bigr)^{2}=\sum_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu^{\prime}}h\bigr)+O(\lambda^{-N}\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}),\,\,\forall\,N.

Note that the
\nu=\theta_{0}\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}^{2(n-1)}
index a
\lambda^{-1/8}
-separated lattice in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2(n-1)}
. As
in earlier works, to be able to apply bilinear oscillatory integral results, we need to organize the pairs
(\nu,\nu^{\prime})
in
the above sum. As in
[
47
]
, we first consider dyadic cubes
\tau^{\theta}_{\mu}
in

{\mathbb{R}}^{2(n-1)}
of sidelength
\theta=2^{k}\theta_{0}=2^{k}\lambda^{-1/8}
, with
\tau^{\theta}_{\mu}
denoting translation of the cube

[0,\theta)^{2(n-1)}
by
\mu=\theta\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}^{2(n-1)}
. We then say two such cubes are
close
if they are not
adjacent but have adjacent parents of sidelength
2\theta
. In this case we write
\tau^{\theta}_{\mu}\sim\tau^{\theta}_{\mu^{\prime}}
. Note that
close cubes satisfy
\text{dist }(\tau^{\theta}_{\mu},\tau^{\theta}_{\mu})\approx\theta
and also that each fixed cube has

O(1)
cubes that are “close” to it. Moreover, as was noted in
[
47
]
, any distinct points

\nu,\nu^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2(n-1)}
must lie in a unique pair of close in this Whitney decomposition of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2(n-1)}
.
Consequently, there must be a unique triple
(\theta=2^{k}\theta_{0},\mu,\mu^{\prime})
such that

(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\in\tau^{\theta}_{\mu}\times\tau^{\theta}_{\mu^{\prime}}
and
\tau^{\theta}_{\mu}\sim\tau^{\theta}_{\mu^{\prime}}
.

We also note that if, as we shall, we fix the
\delta
occurring in the construction of the
\{A_{j,\ell}\}
to be small enough
then we only need to consider
\theta=2^{k}\theta_{0}\ll 1
when dealing with the bilinear sum in (
3.61
).

Based on these observations, we can organize the sum in (
3.61
) as follows

(3.62)

\sum_{\{k\in{\mathbb{N}}:\,k\geq 10\,\,\text{and }\,\theta=2^{k}\theta_{0}\ll 1\}}\sum_{\{(\mu,\mu^{\prime}):\,\tau^{\theta}_{\mu}\sim\tau^{\theta}_{\mu^{\prime}}\}}\sum_{\{(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\in\tau^{\theta}_{\mu}\times\tau^{\theta}_{\mu^{\prime}}\}}\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu^{\prime}}h\bigr)\\
+\sum_{(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\in\Xi_{\theta_{0}}}\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu^{\prime}}h\bigr),

where
\Xi_{0}
indexes the remaining pairs such that
|\nu-\nu^{\prime}|\lesssim\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-1/8}
, including the diagonal ones
where
\nu=\nu^{\prime}
.

Let us then set for our fixed
(j,\ell_{0})
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}=A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}

(3.63)

\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)=\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}(h)=\sum_{(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\in\Xi_{\theta_{0}}}\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu^{\prime}}h\bigr)

and

(3.64)

\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)=\Upsilon^{\text{far}}(h)=\sum_{(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\notin\Xi_{\theta_{0}}}\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu^{\prime}}h\bigr)+O(\lambda^{-N}\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta)}),

with the last term being the error term in (
3.61
).
Due to this splitting we have
the analog of (5.5) in
[
33
]

(3.65)

(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}h)^{2}=\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}(h)+\Upsilon^{\text{far}}(h).

We shall use this decomposition when
n\geq 3
, since then
q_{c}\leq 4
, which allows us to use bilinear ideas
from
[
47
]
, exploiting the fact that
q_{c}/2\in[1,2]
.
When the dimension
n
of
M
equals 2, though, the critical exponent
q_{c}=6
, which, as in
[
4
]
and
[
33
]
,
requires a slight modification of the above splitting.

Specifically, for
n=2
, we first, as in these two earlier works, set

(3.66)

T_{\nu}h=\sum_{\nu^{\prime}:\,(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\in\Xi_{\theta_{0}}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h)(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu^{\prime}}h),

and write

(3.67)

(\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}(h))^{2}=\big(\sum_{\nu}T_{\nu}h\big)^{2}=\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}T_{\nu_{1}}hT_{\nu_{2}}h.

If, as above, we fix
\delta
small enough then the
sum in (
3.67
) can be organized as

(3.68)

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{\{k\in{\mathbb{N}}:\,k\geq 20\,\,\text{and }\,\theta=2^{k}\theta_{0}\ll 1\}}\,\,\sum_{\{(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}):\,\tau^{\theta}_{\mu_{1}}\sim\tau^{\theta}_{\mu_{2}}\}}\sum_{\{(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\in\tau^{\theta}_{\mu_{1}}\times\tau^{\theta}_{\mu_{2}}\}}+\sum_{(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\in\overline{\Xi}_{\theta_{0}}}\big)T_{\nu_{1}}hT_{\nu_{2}}h,

\displaystyle={\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}}(h)+{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{diag}}}(h).

Here
\overline{\Xi}_{\theta_{0}}
indexes the near diagonal pairs. This is another Whitney decomposition similar to
the one in (
3.62
), but the diagonal set
\overline{\Xi}_{\theta_{0}}
is much larger than the set
\Xi_{\theta_{0}}
in (
3.62
). More explicitly, when
n=2
, it is not hard to check that
|\nu-\nu^{\prime}|\leq 2^{11}\theta_{0}
if
(\nu,\nu^{\prime})\in\Xi_{\theta_{0}}

while
|\nu_{1}-\nu_{2}|\leq 2^{21}\theta_{0}
if
(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})\in\overline{\Xi}_{\theta_{0}}
. As noted in
[
33
]
, this helps to simplify the calculations needed for
{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}}(h)
.
Note that for our fixed
(j,\ell_{0})
,

{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{diag}}}(h)={\overline{\Upsilon}_{j,\ell_{0}}^{\text{diag}}}(h)
,
{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}}(h)={\overline{\Upsilon}_{j,\ell_{0}}^{\text{far}}}(h)
,
and
\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)=\Upsilon^{\text{far}}(h)
as in (
3.64
), we then have

(3.69)

(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}h)^{4}\leq 2(\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}h)^{2}+2(\Upsilon^{\text{far}}h)^{2}=2{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{diag}}}(h)+2{\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}}(h)+2(\Upsilon^{\text{far}}(h))^{2},\,\,\text{if }n=2.

We have organized the sums expanding the left side of (
3.61
) exactly as in
[
33
]
. In view of (
3.54
) each of the summands in the above decompositions
is localized to our coordinate chart
\Omega=B(x_{j},2\delta)
on which we are using geodesic normal coordinates about the center. Since our bounded geometry assumptions
ensures we have uniform control of the metric and its derivatives, for
\delta>0
fixed small enough, we can simply repeat the proof of Lemma 5.1 in
[
33
]
to obtain the following
variable coefficient variant of Lemma 6.1 in Tao, Vargas and Vega
[
47
]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-1/8}
with
\lambda\gg 1
.
If
n\geq 3
there is a uniform constant
C=C_{M}
independent of
(j,\ell_{0})
so that
if, as in (
3.51
),
Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}=Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}

(3.70)

\bigl\|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)\bigr\|_{L^{q_{c}/2}(M)}\leq C\bigl(\,\sum_{\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}h\bigr\|^{q_{c}}_{L^{q_{c}}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\,\bigr)^{2/q_{c}}\\
+O(\lambda^{\frac{2}{q_{c}}-}\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}).

Also, for all
n\geq 2
, if
q\in(2,\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n}]
and
\mu(q)
as in (
1.3
), there is a uniform constant

C_{q}=C(q,M)
so that

(3.71)

\bigl\|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)\bigr\|_{L^{q/2}(M)}\leq C_{q}\bigl(\,\sum_{\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}h\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\,\bigr)^{2/q}\\
+O(\lambda^{2\mu(q)-}\|h\|^{2}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}).

Additionally, for
n=2
there is a uniform constant
C=C(M)
so that

(3.72)

\bigl\|\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)\bigr\|_{L^{3/2}(M)}\\
\leq C\bigl(\,\sum_{\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}h\|^{6}_{L^{6}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\bigr)^{2/3}+O(\lambda^{\frac{2}{3}-}\|h\|^{4}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}).

In the above and what follows
O(\lambda^{\mu-})
denotes
O(\lambda^{\mu-\varepsilon_{0}})
for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
.

If we fix
\delta
as well as

\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
in (
3.4
) small enough, then we can use Lee’s
[
38
]
bilinear oscillatory integral theorem
and repeat the proof of Lemma 5.2 in
[
33
]

to obtain the following.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
n\geq 2
and
\Upsilon^{\text{far}}(h)=\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)
be as above with
\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-1/8}
. Then for all
\varepsilon>0
there is a
C_{\varepsilon}=C(\varepsilon,M)
so that

(3.73)

\int_{M}|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)|^{q/2}\,dx\leq C_{\varepsilon}\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\,\bigl(\lambda^{7/8}\bigr)^{\frac{n-1}{2}(q-q_{c})}\|h\|^{q}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))},\quad q=\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n}.

Similarly, for all
n\geq 2
, there is a constant
C_{q}=C(q,M)
so that

(3.74)

\int_{M}|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)|^{q/2}\,dx\leq C_{q}\,\lambda^{q\cdot\mu(q)-}\|h\|^{q}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))},\quad 2<q<\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n},

and, if
n=2
and
\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}(h)=\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)
as in (
3.68
),

(3.75)

\int_{M}|\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(h)|\,dx\leq C_{\varepsilon}\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\lambda^{-7/8}\|h\|^{4}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))},\,\,\forall\,\varepsilon>0,

with
C_{\varepsilon}=C(\varepsilon,M)
.

We now have collected the main ingredients that we need to prove the critical low height estimates.

Proof of (
3.34
).

Let us assume that
n\geq 3
. A main step in the proof of the
A_{-}
estimates then is to obtain the analog of (2.44) in
[
33
]
.
We shall do so largely by repeating its proof, which we do so for the sake of completeness in order to note the small changes needed
to take into account that, unlike (2.44) in
[
33
]
, (
3.34
) here is a vector valued inequality. As noted before, we have taken
this framework to help us exploit our assumption of bounded geometry, and, in particular, the fact that the doubles of the balls
in our covering of
M
,
\{B(x_{j},2\delta)\}
,
have uniformly finite overlap.

We first note that if
q=\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n}<q_{c}
, then by (
3.32
) and (
3.65
) for our fixed
\ell_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\bigl|

\displaystyle\bigl({\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}(\rho_{\lambda}f)(x,j)\bigr)^{2}\bigr|^{q_{c}/2}=\bigl|\bigl(A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)\bigr)^{2}\bigr|^{q_{c}/2}

\displaystyle=|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{\frac{q_{c}-q}{2}}\bigl|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)+\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)\bigr|^{q/2}

\displaystyle\leq|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{\frac{q_{c}-q}{2}}2^{q/2}\bigl(|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{q/2}+|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{q/2}\bigr).

Also, if
A_{-}
is as in (
3.31
),

\|{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|^{q_{c}}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}(A_{-})}=\int_{M}\sum_{j}{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,j)\,|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{q_{c}}\,dx.

Thus,

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|^{q_{c}}_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}(A_{-})}=\sum_{j}\int{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,j)\,|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{q_{c}/2}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{j}\int\bigl[{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,j)\,|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{\frac{q_{c}-q}{2}}\bigr]\,|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{q/2}\,dx

\displaystyle\,\,+C\sum_{j}\int\bigl[{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,j)\,|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{\frac{q_{c}-q}{2}}\bigr]\,|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(\rho_{\lambda}f)(x)|^{q/2}\,dx

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=C(I+II).

To handle
II
we recall that by (
3.31
), (
3.32
) and (
3.54
)

|{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,j)A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f(x)|\leq\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\frac{1}{8}}.

Thus, by (
3.73
),

\displaystyle CII

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{(\frac{n-1}{4}+\frac{1}{8})(q_{c}-q)}\cdot\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\,(\lambda^{\frac{7}{8}})^{\frac{n-1}{2}(q-q_{c})}\sum_{j}\|\rho_{\lambda}f\|^{q}_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{1-\delta_{n}+\varepsilon}\|\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{q}\leq\lambda^{1-\delta_{n}+\varepsilon}\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}^{q}=\lambda^{1-\delta_{n}+\varepsilon},

using also, in the second inequality, the bounded overlap of the
\{B(x_{j},2\delta)\}
. Also, a simple calculation shows that
\delta_{n}>0
.

To control
CI
, as in
[
4
]
and
[
33
]
, we use Hölder’s inequality and Young’s inequality along with (
3.70
) to get

\displaystyle CI

\displaystyle\leq\|{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\cdot{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|_{L^{q_{c}/2}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}/2}(A_{-})}^{\frac{q_{c}-q}{2}}\cdot C\|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(\rho_{\lambda}f)\|^{q/2}_{L^{q_{c}/2}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{q_{c}-q}{q_{c}}\|{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\cdot{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|_{L^{q_{c}/2}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}/2}(A_{-})}^{q_{c}/2}+\tfrac{q}{q_{c}}C\|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(\rho_{\lambda}f)\|^{q_{c}/2}_{L^{q_{c}/2}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}/2}}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{q_{c}-q}{q_{c}}\|{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}(A_{-})}^{q_{c}}

\displaystyle\qquad+C^{\prime}\tfrac{q}{q_{c}}\bigl[\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|^{q_{c}}_{L^{q_{c}}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}+\lambda^{1-}\bigl(\sum_{j}\|\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{2}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\bigr)^{q_{c}/2}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{q_{c}-q}{q_{c}}\|{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}(A_{-})}^{q_{c}}+C^{\prime\prime}\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|^{q_{c}}_{L^{q_{c}}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}+\lambda^{1-},

again using the finite overlap of the
\{B(x_{j},2\delta)\}
.

Since
\tfrac{q_{c}-q}{q_{c}}<1
we can use the bounds for
CI
and
CII
to obtain the key inequality

\|{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{-})}\lesssim\bigl(\,\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}(M)}^{q_{c}}\bigr)^{1/q_{c}}+\lambda^{\frac{1}{q_{c}}-},

which is the analog of the estimate (2.44) in Proposition 2.3 in
[
33
]
for
n\geq 3
. One can similarly use (
3.72
) and (
3.75
) and
modify the arguments in
[
33
]

to handle the two-dimensional case. Similarly, if one also uses (
3.74
) one can obtain an analog of the preceding
estimate for subcritical exponents,
2<q<q_{c}
, which is more straightforward and does not require the norm in the left
to be taken over
A_{-}
.

Thus, just as the preceding inequality followed from straightforward modifications of the arguments in
[
33
]
, so do
the other estimates in the following result coming from variable coefficient variants of the bilinear harmonic
analysis in
[
47
]
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Fix a complete
n\geq 2
Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
of bounded geometry and assume that (
3.30
) is valid.
Then
\lambda\gg 1
and
\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-1/8}

(3.76)

\|{\mathcal{A}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|_{L^{q_{c}}_{x}\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}(A_{-})}\lesssim\bigl(\,\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}(M)}^{q_{c}}\bigr)^{1/q_{c}}+\lambda^{\frac{1}{q_{c}}-},

assuming that
\delta
and
\delta_{1}
above are small enough. Additionally, for
2<q\leq\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n}
,

(3.77)

\bigl(\sum_{j}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f)\|_{L^{q}_{x}(M)}^{q}\bigr)^{1/q}\lesssim\bigl(\,\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q}(M)}^{q}\bigr)^{1/q}+\lambda^{\mu(q)-}.

Due to (
3.76
), in order to prove (
3.34
) and finish the proof of the
q_{c}
-estimates
in Theorem
1.5
, it suffices to show that if, as above, we take
T=c_{0}\log\lambda
as in (
3.33
), then

(3.78)

\bigl(\,\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}(M)}^{q_{c}}\bigr)^{1/q_{c}}\lesssim\lambda^{\mu(q_{c})}T^{-1/2},\\
\text{if all the sectional curvatures of }M\,\text{are }\leq-\kappa_{0}^{2},\,\text{some }\kappa_{0}>0,

and

(3.79)

\bigl(\,\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{q_{c}}(M)}^{q_{c}}\bigr)^{1/q_{c}}\lesssim\bigl(\lambda T^{-1}\bigr)^{\mu(q_{c})},\\
\text{if all the sectional curvatures of }M\,\text{are nonpositive}.

To prove these two estimates we shall argue as in the proof of (2.56) in
[
33
]
and use (
3.8
), (
3.20
)
and (
3.57
) to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{j,\nu}

\displaystyle\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}=\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{2}\cdot\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}-2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{2}\cdot\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}-2}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{2}\cdot\|\,(A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}-A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda})\rho_{\lambda}f\|^{q_{c}-2}_{q_{c}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{2}\cdot\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}-2}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{j,\nu}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\lambda^{(\frac{1}{q_{c}}-\frac{1}{4})(q_{c}-2)}\|\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{2}^{q_{c}-2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{2}\cdot\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}-2}+\lambda^{1-\frac{1}{4}(q_{c}-2)}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}\bigr)^{\frac{2}{q_{c}}}\bigl(\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}\bigr)^{\frac{q_{c}-2}{q_{c}}}+C\lambda^{1-\frac{1}{4}(q_{c}-2)}.

By Young’s inequality, the second to last term is bounded for any
\kappa>0
by

C\Bigl[\,\frac{2}{q_{c}}\kappa^{\frac{q_{c}}{2}}\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}+\frac{q_{c}-2}{q_{c}}\kappa^{-\frac{q_{c}}{q_{c}-2}}\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}\,\Bigr].

If
\kappa
is small enough the first term here is smaller than half of the left side of the preceding inequality.
So, by an absorbing argument and the fact that
q_{c}>2
, we conclude that

\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}\lesssim\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}+\lambda^{1-}.

If we next use (
3.20
), (
3.55
), followed by (
3.12
) we find that we can
control the first term in the right as follows

\displaystyle\sum_{j,\nu}\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j,\nu}\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j,\nu}\Bigl[\,\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}+\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}(I-\sigma_{\lambda})\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j,\nu}\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}+\|(I-\sigma_{\lambda})\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{j,\nu}\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f\|_{q_{c}}^{q_{c}}+\lambda\cdot(\log\lambda)^{-q_{c}}.

If we combine (
3.76
) and the preceding two inequalities we conclude that we would obtain (
3.33
) and consequently
finish the proof of the estimates in Theorem
1.5
if, for
T
as in (
3.9
), we could show that

(3.80)

Uf(x,j,\nu)=(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\rho_{\lambda}f)(x),

satisfies

\|Uf\|_{\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}\ell_{\nu}^{q_{c}}L^{q_{c}}_{x}}\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{1}{q_{c}}}T^{-1/2}\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}

if all the sectional curvatures of
(M,g)
are
\leq-\kappa_{0}^{2}
for some
\kappa_{0}>0
as well
as

\|Uf\|_{\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}\ell_{\nu}^{q_{c}}L^{q_{c}}_{x}}\lesssim\bigl(\lambda T^{-1}\bigr)^{\frac{1}{q_{c}}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}

if all the sectional curvatures of
(M,g)
are nonpositive. Equivalently, this would be a consequence
of the following

(3.81)

\|UU^{*}\|_{\ell_{j^{\prime}}^{q_{c}^{\prime}}\ell_{\nu^{\prime}}^{q_{c}^{\prime}}L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}\to\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}\ell^{q_{c}}_{\nu}L^{q_{c}}_{x}}\\
\lesssim\begin{cases}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}T^{-1},\,\,\text{if all the sectional curvatures of }M\,\text{are }\leq-\kappa_{0}^{2},\,\text{some }\kappa_{0}>0,\\
\bigl(\lambda T^{-1}\bigr)^{2/q_{c}}\,\,\text{if all the sectional curvatures of }M\,\text{are nonpositive}.\end{cases}

To prove the large height
A_{+}
estimates (
3.33
) we split
\rho_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}^{*}=L_{\lambda}+G_{\lambda}
as in
(
3.38
). To prove (
3.81
), we require an additional dyadic decomposition, as well as taking into account
the second microlocal decomposition afforded by the
\{Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell,\nu}\}
. To obtain this dyadic decomposition, we
fix a Littlewood-Paley bump function
\beta\in C^{\infty}_{0}((1/2,2))
satisfying
\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\beta(s/2^{k})=1
,

s>0
. If we let
\beta_{0}(t)=1-\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\beta(|t|/2^{k})
, then
\beta_{0}\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
equals one near the
origin, and so plays the role of
a(t)
in (
3.37
). So, analogous to (
3.37
), we set

L_{\lambda,T}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\beta_{0}(t)\,T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\,e^{it\lambda}\,e^{-itP}\,dt,

with, as in Lemma
3.1
,
\Psi=|\rho|^{2}
.

If then
G_{\lambda}=G_{\lambda,T}
is as in (
3.35
) with
a=\beta_{0}
, we use the dyadic decomposition given by

(3.82)

G_{\lambda,T,N}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\beta(|t|/N)\,T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\,e^{it\lambda}\,e^{-itP}\,dt,

so that, if we consider the resulting dyadic sum, we have

(3.83)

G_{\lambda}=\sum_{1\leq 2^{k}=N\lesssim T}G_{\lambda,T,N}.

Then, if we set,

(3.84)

\bigl(W_{N}F\bigr)(x,j,\nu)=\sum_{j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\circ G_{\lambda,T,N}\circ\bigl(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j^{\prime},\ell_{0},\nu^{\prime}}\bigr)^{*}F(x,j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})

by (
3.38
), (
3.80
) and (
3.83
) we have

(3.85)

\bigl(UU^{*}F)(x,j,\nu)=\sum_{j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}\bigl(\,Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\circ L_{\lambda,T}\circ(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j^{\prime},\ell_{0},\nu^{\prime}})^{*}\bigr)F(x,j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\\
+\sum_{1\leq N=2^{k}\lesssim T}\bigl(W_{N}F\bigr)(x,j,\nu).

The operator
L_{\lambda,T}
satisfies the bounds in (
3.39
). If we use this along with the first inequality
in (
3.56
) for
q=q_{c}
followed by this bound and then the second inequality for
p=q_{c}^{\prime}
in
(
3.55
) we obtain

\Bigl\|\sum_{j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}\bigl(\,Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\circ L_{\lambda,T}\circ(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j^{\prime},\ell_{0},\nu^{\prime}})^{*}\bigr)F(\,\cdot\,,j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\Bigr\|_{\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}\ell^{q_{c}}_{\nu}L^{q_{c}}_{x}}\leq C\lambda^{2/q_{c}}T^{-1}\|F\|_{\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}},

which agrees with the bounds in (
3.81
) for strictly negative sectional curvatures and is better than the bounds
posited for nonpositive curvature.

To obtain the desired bounds for the last term in (
3.85
), we shall require the following result which plays here
the role of Lemma
3.1
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
G_{\lambda,T,N}
be as in (
3.82
). Then for
\lambda\gg 1
and
\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-1/8}
we have the uniform bounds

(3.86)

\bigl\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}G_{\lambda,T,N}\bigr\|_{L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C_{M}T^{-1}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}N^{1-\frac{n-1}{2}},\,\,N\geq 1,

if
(M,g)
is a complete manifold of bounded geometry all of whose sectional curvatures are nonpositive and

T=c_{0}\log\lambda
is fixed with
c_{0}=c_{0}(M)>0
sufficiently small Moreover, if we assume that all of the sectional curvatures
are
\leq-\kappa_{0}^{2}
, some
\kappa_{0}>0
, then we have the uniform bounds

(3.87)

\bigl\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}G_{\lambda,T,N}\bigr\|_{L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C_{M}T^{-1}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}N^{-m},\,\,\forall\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Like those in Lemma
3.1
, these two bounds follow from kernel estimates which we shall obtain at the end of this section.

Let us now see how we can use this lemma to see that the last term in the right side of (
3.85
) satisfies the
bounds in (
3.81
).

We first notice that, by (
3.82
), the operators in (
3.82
) have
O(T^{-1}N)

L^{2}(M)\to L^{2}(M)
operator norms.
Thus, by (
3.56
) for
q=p=2
(almost orthogonality), we have, by (
3.84
)

(3.88)

\bigl\|W_{N}\bigr\|_{\ell^{2}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{2}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{2}_{x}\to\ell^{2}_{j}\ell^{2}_{\nu}L^{2}_{x}}=O(T^{-1}N).

If we use the second inequality in (
3.56
) for
p=1
along with (
3.84
)
and (
3.86
), we also obtain that for
T
as above and
N=2^{k}\geq 1

(3.89)

\|W_{N}\|_{\ell^{1}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{1}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{1}_{x}\to\ell^{\infty}_{j}\ell^{\infty}_{\nu}L^{\infty}_{x}}=O(T^{-1}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}N^{1-\frac{n-1}{2}})

if all of the sectional curvatures of
M
are nonpositive, as well as

(3.90)

\|W_{N}\|_{\ell^{1}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{1}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{1}_{x}\to\ell^{\infty}_{j}\ell^{\infty}_{\nu}L^{\infty}_{x}}=O(T^{-1}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}N^{-m}),\,\,\forall\,m\in{\mathbb{N}},

if all of the principal curvatures are pinched below zero as in (
3.87
).

If we interpolate between (
3.88
) and (
3.90
) we obtain

(3.91)

\|W_{N}\|_{\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}\to\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}\ell^{q_{c}}_{\nu}L^{q_{c}}_{x}}=O(T^{-1}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}N^{1-m}),\,\,\forall\,m\in{\mathbb{N}},

if all of the sectional curvatures of
M
are
\leq-\kappa_{0}^{2}
, some
\kappa_{0}>0
. As a result, we can estimate the
last term in (
3.85
) as follows

(3.92)

\Bigl\|\sum_{1\leq N=2^{k}\lesssim T}W_{N}F\Bigr\|_{\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}\ell^{q_{c}}_{\nu}L^{q_{c}}_{x}}\lesssim T^{-1}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}\sum_{1=N\lesssim T}N^{-1}\|F\|_{\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}}\\
\lesssim T^{-1}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}\|F\|_{\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}},

and so this term also satisfies the bounds in (
3.81
).

If we merely assume that all of the sectional curvatures are nonpositive, then (
3.88
), (
3.89
) and interpolation yield

\|W_{N}\|_{\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}\to\ell^{q_{c}}_{j}\ell^{q_{c}}_{\nu}L^{q_{c}}_{x}}=O(T^{-1}\lambda^{2/q_{c}}N^{1-\frac{n-1}{n+1}}),\,\,\forall\,m\in{\mathbb{N}},

Since
\tfrac{n-1}{n+1}=\tfrac{2}{q_{c}}
, we therefore obtain

(3.93)

\Bigl\|\sum_{1\leq N=2^{k}\lesssim T}W_{N}F\Bigr\|_{\ell_{j}^{q_{c}}\ell^{q_{c}}_{\nu}L^{q_{c}}_{x}}\lesssim(\lambda T^{-1})^{2/q_{c}}\|F\|_{\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{j^{\prime}}\ell^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{q_{c}^{\prime}}_{x}},

as desired under this curvature assumption.

Inequalities (
3.92
), (
3.93
) along with the earlier bounds for the first term in (
3.85
) yield (
3.81
).
As a result, except for needing to prove Lemmas
3.1
and
3.5
, the proof of the bounds in
Theorem
1.5
for
q=q_{c}
is complete.

Since we also earlier obtained the bounds for
q\in(q_{c},\infty]
, it only remains to obtain the bounds for

q\in(2,q_{c})
. If the curvatures are assumed to be nonpositive, then the bounds in (
1.12
) for these exponents
just follow from interpolating between the bounds for
q=q_{c}
and the trivial
L^{2}
-estimate. So, to complete the proof
of the Theorem, by (
3.12
) it suffices to show that for
T
as above we have

\|\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(M)\to L^{q}(M)}=O(T^{-1/2}\lambda^{\mu(q)}),\,\,q\in(2,q_{c})

when all the sectional curvatures of
M
are pinched below zero and
T
is as above. By interpolating with the

q=q_{c}
estimate that we just obtained, it suffices to show that, under these assumptions, we have

(3.94)

\|\sigma_{\lambda}\rho_{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}(M)\to L^{q}(M)}=O(T^{-1/2}\lambda^{\mu(q)}),\,\,q\in(2,\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n}].

This just follows from the above arguments which gave us the bounds in (
3.34
) for
q=q_{c}
under this curvature assumption
if we use (
3.77
) in place of (
3.76
). The argument is a bit simpler since the norms in the left side of
(
3.77
) are over
M
. So, we do not need for this case to split
M=A_{-}\cup A_{+}
to handle the exponents in (
3.94
).
∎

3.2. Log-scale Strichartz estimates

In this section, let us see how we can follow the ideas in the previous section to adapt the proofs for the compact manifold case treated in
[
5
,
32
]

to prove Theorem
1.3
.

To align with the numerology in the previous section on the spectral projection estimates, throughout this section,
we shall always assume the manifold is
(n-1)
-dimensional.
Additionally, we will repeatedly use symbols such as
\sigma_{\lambda}
and
Q_{\nu}^{\theta}
; however, it is important to note that they represent different operators in this section.

To start, let us fix

(3.95)

\eta\in C^{\infty}_{0}((-1,1))\quad\text{with }\,\,\eta(t)=1,\,\,\,|t|\leq 1/2.

We shall consider the dyadic time-localized dilated Schrödinger operators

(3.96)

S_{\lambda}=\eta(t/T)e^{-it\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}\beta(P/\lambda),

where
\beta\in C^{\infty}_{0}((1/2,2))
as in (
1.9
) and
T=c_{0}\log\lambda
for some small constant
c_{0}
we shall specify later. By changing scale in time, to prove Theorem
1.3

it suffices to show that if all of the sectional curvatures of
M
are nonpositive then for
(p,q)
satisfying (
1.10
) we have

(3.97)

\|S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}(M\times[0,T])}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)},\quad\text{if }\,\,T=c_{0}\log\lambda.

Note that if we replace
[0,T]
by
[0,1]
, then by using the analog of (
1.9
) for intervals
[0,\lambda^{-1}]
along with a rescaling argument, we have for any complete manifold of bounded geometry

(3.98)

\|S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}(M\times[0,1])}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}\,\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

Now we shall introduce the auxiliary operators that allow us to use bilinear
techniques. Let
\rho\in{\mathcal{S}}({\mathbb{R}})
satisfying (
3.4
), we define the local operators

(3.99)

\sigma_{\lambda}=\left(\rho\bigl(\lambda^{1/2}|D_{t}|^{1/2}-P\bigr)+\rho\bigl(\lambda^{1/2}|D_{t}|^{1/2}+P\bigr)\right)\,\tilde{\beta}(D_{t}/\lambda),

where

(3.100)

\tilde{\beta}\in C^{\infty}_{0}((1/8,8))\quad\text{satisfies }\,\,\tilde{\beta}=1\,\,\text{on }\,\,[1/6,6].

Note that by by Euler’s formula,

(3.101)

\sigma_{\lambda}(x,t;y,s)=\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\iint e^{i(t-s)\tau}e^{ir\lambda^{1/2}\tau^{1/2}}\tilde{\beta}(\tau/\lambda)\,\hat{\rho}(r)\,\cos(rP)(x,y)\,drd\tau.

Thus by the support properties of
\hat{\rho}
as in (
3.4
) and finite propagation speed, we have

(3.102)

\sigma_{\lambda}(x,t;y,s)=0\quad\text{if }\,\,d_{g}(x,y)>r,\,\,r=\delta_{1}(1+\delta_{2})<1.

For admissible pairs
(p,q)
as in (
1.10
), the local
operators satisfy

(3.103)

\|(I-\sigma_{\lambda})\circ S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}(M\times[0,T])}\leq CT^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}\|f\|_{2},

This is a straightforward generalization of Lemma 2.2 in
[
5
]
to all complete manifold of bounded geometry and all
pairs
(p,q)
satisfying (
1.10
). The proof relies on the local dyadic Strichartz estimates (
3.98
) along with the spectral theorem and functional calculus for multiplier operators. We skip the details here and refer to
[
5
]

for more details.

For each fixed
j
