, if we use the microlocal pseudodifferential operators
A_{j,\ell}
defined in (
3.17
), we can write

(3.104)

\psi_{j}(x)(\sigma_{\lambda}F)(t,x)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{K}\bigl(A_{j,\ell}\circ\sigma_{\lambda}\bigr)(F)(t,x)+R_{j}F(t,x).

where as in (
3.16
)

(3.105)

A_{j,\ell}(x,y),\,\,R_{j}(x,t;y,s)=0,\quad\text{if }\,x\notin B(x_{j},\delta)\,\,\text{or }\,\,y\notin B(x_{j},3\delta/2).

As before, by fixing
c_{1}>0
small enough in the symbol of
A_{j,\ell}
operators,
we have the uniform
bounds

(3.106)

R_{j}(x,t;y,s)=O(\lambda^{-N}),\,\,N=1,2,...

As in the previous section, the microlocal operators
A_{j,\ell}
will be useful in the local harmonic analysis arguments we shall describe later.
Note also that by (
3.2
), (
3.105
) and (
3.106
) we also have

(3.107)

\|RF\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}(M\times[0,T])}\leq C_{p,q,M}\,\|F\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}(M\times{\mathbb{R}})},\,\,1\leq p\leq q\leq\infty,\,\,\text{if },\,\,R=\sum_{j}R_{j}.

Note that for fixed
\ell_{0}
, if we let
A=\sum_{j}A_{j,\ell_{0}}
as in (
3.21
), in view of (
3.3
), (
3.103
), (
3.104
) and (
3.107
), in order
to prove (
3.97
), it suffices to
prove that if all the sectional curvatures of

M
are nonpositive

(3.108)

\|A\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}(M\times[0,T])}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}\|f\|_{2}.

And if we consider the vector-valued operators

(3.109)

{\mathcal{A}}H(x,t)=(A_{1,\ell_{0}}H(x,t),\,A_{2,\ell_{0}}H(x,t),\dots)

and argue as in (
3.25
)-(
3.27
), (
3.108
) would be a consequence of

(3.110)

\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}({\mathbb{N}}\times M\times[0,T])}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}\|f\|_{2}.

The operators
{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}
play the role of the
\tilde{S}_{\lambda}
operators in
[
5
]
and
[
32
]
. As in the previous section,
the vector-valued approach will allow us to only have to carry out the local bilinear harmonic analysis
in individual coordinate patches coming from the geodesic normal coordinates in the balls
B(x_{j},2\delta)
.

Now let’s set up the height decomposition that we shall use, throughout this section, we assume

(3.111)

\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}=1.

Let us
define vector-valued sets

(3.112)

\begin{split}A_{+}&=\{(x,t,j)\in M\times[0,T]\times{\mathbb{N}}:\,|({\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f)(x,t,j)|\geq\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\varepsilon_{1}}\}\\
A_{-}&=\{(x,t,j)\in M\times[0,T]\times{\mathbb{N}}:\,|({\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f)(x,t,j)|<\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\varepsilon_{1}}\}.\end{split}

Recall here that

(3.113)

({\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f)(x,t,j)=A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f(x,t).

Due to the numerology of the powers of
\lambda
arising,
the splitting occurs at height
\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\varepsilon_{1}}
, with
\frac{n-1}{4}
same as the previous section. Here
\varepsilon_{1}>0
is a small constant that may depend on the dimension
n-1
. As we shall see later, we can take
\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{1}{100}
for
n-1\geq 3
while for
n-1=2
, the choice of
\varepsilon_{1}
depends on the exponent
q
for
admissible
pairs
(p,q)
, with
\varepsilon_{1}\rightarrow 0
as
q\rightarrow\infty
.

In order to prove (
3.110
) on the set
A_{+}
, we shall require the following lemma

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
S_{t,\lambda}
denote the operator

\eta(t/T)\beta(P/\lambda)e^{-it\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}
.
Then if
M
has nonpositive sectional curvatures and
T=c_{0}\log\lambda
with

c_{0}=c_{0}(M)>0
sufficiently small, we have for
\lambda\gg 1

(3.114)

\|S_{t,\lambda}S^{*}_{s,\lambda}\|_{L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\exp(C_{M}|t-s|).

We shall postpone the proof of this lemma until the end of this section and first see how we can use it to prove (
3.110
) on the set
A_{+}
.

Proof of (
3.110
) on the set
A_{+}
.

We first note that, by (
3.103
), (
3.109
) and (
3.111
),
we have

\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}(A_{+})}\leq\|{\mathcal{A}}S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}(A_{+})}+CT^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Since
p\geq 2
for
(p,q)
as in (
1.10
), (
3.110
) would follow from

(3.115)

\|{\mathcal{A}}S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}(A_{+})}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}(A_{+})}.

To prove this, similar to what was done in
[
32
]
, we choose
g=g(x,t,j)
such that

(3.116)

\|g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q^{\prime}}_{j}(A_{+})}=1\,\,\text{and }\,\,\|{\mathcal{A}}S_{\lambda}f\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}(A_{+})}\\
=\sum_{j}\iint{\mathcal{A}}S_{\lambda}f(x,t,j)\cdot\overline{\bigl({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g(x,t,j)\bigr)}\,dxdt.

Then, since we are assuming that
\|f\|_{2}=1
, by
the Schwarz inequality

(3.117)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{A}}S_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}(A_{+})}

\displaystyle=\Bigl(\,\int f(x)\,\cdot\,\overline{\bigl(S_{\lambda}^{*}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\bigr)({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\bigr)(x)}\,dx\,\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int|S^{*}_{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x)|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\iint\bigl({\mathcal{A}}S_{\lambda}S^{*}_{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\bigr)({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,t,j)\,\overline{({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,t,j)}\,dxdt

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\iint\bigl({\mathcal{A}}\circ L_{\lambda}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\bigr)({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,t,j)\,\overline{({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,t,j)}\,dxdt

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{j}\iint\bigl({\mathcal{A}}\circ G_{\lambda}\circ{\mathcal{A}}^{*}\bigr)({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,t,j)\,\overline{({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)(x,t,j)}\,dxdt

\displaystyle=I+II,

where
L_{\lambda}
is the integral operator with kernel equaling that of
S_{t,\lambda}S^{*}_{s,\lambda}
if
|t-s|\leq 1
and

0
otherwise, i.e,

(3.118)

L_{\lambda}(x,t;y,s)=\begin{cases}S_{t,\lambda}S^{*}_{s,\lambda}(x,y),\,\,\text{if }\,\,|t-s|\leq 1,\\
0\,\,\,\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Since
p\geq 2
, it is straightforward to see that (
3.98
) and (
3.114
)
yield

(3.119)

\|L_{\lambda}\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}\to L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}=O(\lambda^{\frac{2}{p}}).

If we use this, along with Hölder’s inequality, (
3.27
) and
(
3.116
), we obtain for the term
I
in (
3.117
)

(3.120)

\displaystyle|I|

\displaystyle\leq\|{\mathcal{A}}L_{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}}\cdot\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|L_{\lambda}{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}}\cdot\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q^{\prime}}_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{2}{p}}\|{\mathcal{A}}^{*}({\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g)\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q^{\prime}}_{j}}\cdot\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q^{\prime}}_{j}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{2}{p}}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q^{\prime}}_{j}(A_{+})}=\lambda^{\frac{2}{p}}.

To estimate
II
, note that
if we choose
c_{0}
small enough
so that if
C_{M}
is the constant
in (
3.114
)

\exp(2C_{M}T)\leq\lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}},\quad\text{if }\,T=c_{0}\log\lambda\,\,\text{and }\,\,\lambda\gg 1.

Then, since
\eta(t)=0
for
|t|\geq 1
, it follows
(
3.114
) that

\|G_{\lambda}\|_{L^{1}(M\times{\mathbb{R}})\to L^{\infty}(M\times{\mathbb{R}})}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}+{\varepsilon_{1}}}.

As a result, by Hölder’s inequality (
3.27
) and
(
3.116
), we can repeat the arguments
to estimate
I
to see that

(3.121)

|II|\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\cdot g\|^{2}_{L^{1}_{t,x}\ell^{1}_{j}}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\|g\|^{2}_{L^{p^{\prime}}_{t}L^{q^{\prime}}_{x}\ell^{q^{\prime}}_{j}}\cdot\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}}^{2}\\
=C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\lambda^{\varepsilon_{1}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}}^{2}.

If we recall the definition of
A_{+}
in
(
3.112
), we can estimate the last
factor:

\|{\bf 1}_{A_{+}}\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}}^{2}\leq\bigl(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\varepsilon_{1}}\bigr)^{-2}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}}\ell^{q}_{j}(A_{+})}.

Therefore,

|II|\lesssim\lambda^{-\varepsilon_{1}}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\|^{2}_{{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}}(A_{+})}\leq\bigl(\tfrac{1}{2}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\|_{{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell^{q}_{j}}(A_{+})}\bigr)^{2},

assuming, as we may, that
\lambda
is large enough.

If we combine this bound with the earlier one,
(
3.120
) for
I
, we conclude that
(
3.115
) is valid, which completes the
proof of (
3.110
) on the set
A_{+}
.
∎

Next, we shall give the proof of (
3.110
) on the set
A_{-}
. This requires the use of local bilinear harmonic
analysis. Following the approach in the previous section, in view of (
3.104
), it suffices to carry out the analysis in geodesic normal coordinates of each individual balls
B(x_{j},2\delta)
, since our assumption of bounded geometry ensures
bounded transition maps and uniform bounds on derivatives of the metric. Also note that since the case
p=\infty,q=2
in (
3.97
) simply follows from spectral theorem, to prove (
3.110
) on the set
A_{-}
, for the remaining of this section, we shall assume

(3.122)

(p,q)=(2,\tfrac{2(n-1)}{n-3})\,\,\text{if }\,n\geq 4,\,\,\,\text{or }\,(n-1)(\tfrac{1}{2}-\tfrac{1}{q})=\tfrac{2}{p},\,\,4<q<\infty\,\,\text{if }\,\,n=3.

The condition
q>4
is equivalent to
q>p
when
n-1=2
,
this will allow us to simplify some of the calculations to follow.

To set up
the second microlocalization needed for the Schrödinger setting, let us fix
j
in (
3.15
), as well as
\ell_{0}\in\{1,\dots,K\}
and consider the resulting
pseudodifferential cutoff,
A_{j,\ell_{0}}
, which is a summand in (
3.104
). Its symbol then satisfies
the conditions in (
3.18
). The resulting geodesic normal coordinates on
B(x_{j},2\delta)

vanish at
x_{j}
. We may also
assume that
\xi_{j,\ell_{0}}=(0,\dots,0,1)
. Since we are fixing

j
and
\ell_{0}
for now, analogous to
[
32
]
, let us simplify the notation a bit by letting

(3.123)

\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}=A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda},

The
Q_{\nu}^{\theta}
operators constructed in the last section provide “directional” microlocalization. We
also need a “height” localization since the characteristics of the symbols of our scaled Schrödinger operators lie on
paraboloids. The variable coefficient operators that we shall use are analogs of ones that are used in the study of Fourier restriction
problems involving paraboloids.

To construct these, choose
b\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
supported in
|s|\leq 1
satisfying
\sum_{-\infty}^{\infty}b(s-\ell)\equiv 1
.
We then define the compound symbols
Q^{\theta}_{\ell}=Q^{\theta}_{j,\ell_{0},\ell}
and associated “height” operators by

(3.124)

Q^{\theta}_{\ell}(x,y,\xi)=\tilde{\tilde{\psi}}(y)b(\theta^{-1}\lambda^{-1}(p(x,\xi)-\lambda\kappa^{\theta}_{\ell})),\,\,\kappa^{\theta}_{\ell}=1+\theta\ell,\quad|\ell|\lesssim\theta^{-1},\\
\text{and}\,\,\,Q^{\theta}_{\ell}h(x)=(2\pi)^{-(n-1)}\iint e^{i(x-y)\cdot\xi}Q^{\theta}_{\ell}(x,y,\xi)\,h(y)\,d\xi dy.

Here
\tilde{\tilde{\psi}}\in C^{\infty}_{0}
is supported in

|x|<2\delta
which equals one when
|x|\leq 3\delta/2
, as defined in (
3.51
).

Unlike in the earlier works
[
32
,
5
]
, the height operators here are defined in local coordinates and have cutoffs in
y
variable in order to avoid issues at infinity since
M
is not assumed to be compact. In the compact case, the analogous height operator can be simply defined using spectral multipliers, see e.g.,
[
5
,
32
]
.
These operators microlocalize
p(x,\xi)
to
intervals of size
\approx\theta\lambda
about “heights”

\lambda\kappa^{\theta}_{\ell}\approx\lambda
. By a simple integration by parts argument, if
Q^{\theta}_{\ell}(x,y)
is the kernel of this operator then

(3.125)

Q^{\theta}_{\ell}(x,y)=O(\lambda^{-N})\,\forall\,N,\quad\text{if }\,d_{g}(x,y)\geq C_{0}\theta,

for a fixed constant
C_{0}
if
\theta\in[\lambda^{-1/2+\varepsilon},1]
with
\varepsilon>0
.

For
\nu=(\nu^{\prime},\ell)=(\theta k,\theta\ell)\in\theta{\mathbb{Z}}^{2(n-2)+1}
we now define the cutoffs that we shall use:

(3.126)

Q^{\theta}_{\nu}=Q^{\theta}_{\nu^{\prime}}\circ Q^{\theta}_{\ell}.

where
Q^{\theta}_{\nu^{\prime}}
are the directional microlocalization operators defined in (
3.51
). Both
Q^{\theta}_{\nu^{\prime}}
and
Q^{\theta}_{\ell}
operators here depend on our fixed
j,\ell_{0}
, and as in (
3.52
), due to the way they are constructed, for small enough
\delta_{0}>0
the principle symbol
q^{\theta}_{\nu}(x,y,\xi)
of the
Q_{\nu}^{\theta}
operators satisfy

(3.127)

q^{\theta}_{\nu}(x,y,\xi)=q^{\theta}_{\nu}(z,y,\eta),\,(z,\eta)=\Phi_{t}(x,\xi),\\
\text{if }\,\text{dist }((x,\xi),\,\text{supp }A_{j,\ell_{0}})\leq\delta_{0}\,\text{and }\,\,|t|\leq 2\delta_{0}.

The symbol of
Q_{\nu}^{\theta}
operators in (
3.126
) vanishes when either
x
or
y
is outside the
2\delta
-ball about
the origin in our coordinates for
\Omega
. By (
3.101
) (
3.105
) and (
3.123
), we can fix
\delta_{1}
in (
3.4
) small enough so that
we also have, analogous to (2.39) in
[
32
]
,

(3.128)

\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}=\sum_{\nu}\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}+R,\quad R=R_{\lambda,j,\ell_{0}},\,\,\,\,\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}=A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda},\\
\text{where }\,\,R(x,t;y,s)=O(\lambda^{-N}),\,\forall\,\,N\,\,\,\\
\text{and }R(x,t;y,s)=0,\,\,\text{if }x\notin B(x_{j},2\delta)\,\,\text{or }y\notin B(x_{j},2\delta),

with bounds for the remainder kernel independent of
j
. Here unlike in the previous section we take
\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-\varepsilon_{0}}
for some small constant
\varepsilon_{0}

that we shall specify later, the choice of
\varepsilon_{0}
depend on the dimension
n-1
.

Let us now point out straightforward but useful properties of our operators. First, by (
3.105
), (
3.128
)
and the support properties of
\tilde{\psi}
,
\tilde{\tilde{\psi}}
, we have

(3.129)

\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}H={\bf 1}_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}\cdot\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}\bigl({\bf 1}_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}\cdot H\bigr),\,\,Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}=Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\\
\text{and }\,\,RH={\bf 1}_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}\cdot R\bigl({\bf 1}_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}\cdot H\bigr),\,\,R=R_{\lambda,j,\ell_{0}}.

Also, we have the uniform bounds

(3.130)

\begin{split}\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}h\|_{\ell^{q}_{\nu}L^{q}(M)}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{q}(M)},\,\,2\leq q\leq\infty\\
\bigl\|\sum_{\nu^{\prime}}(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu})^{*}H(\nu^{\prime},\,\cdot\,)\|_{L^{p}(M)}\lesssim\|H\|_{\ell^{p}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{p}(M)},\,\,1\leq p\leq 2.\end{split}

The second estimate follows via duality from the first. The first one is the analog of (2.42) in
[
32
]
. By interpolation,
one just needs to verify that the estimate holds for the two endpoints,
p=2
and
p=\infty
. The former follows via an almost
orthogonality argument, and the latter from the fact that
for each
x
the symbols vanish outside of cubes of sidelength
\theta\lambda
and

|\partial^{\gamma}_{\xi}Q^{\theta}_{\nu}(x,y,\xi)|=O((\lambda\theta)^{-|\gamma|})
, thus it is not hard to show we have the uniform bounds

\sup_{x\in B(x_{j},2\delta)}\int_{B(x_{j},2\delta)}|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}(x,y)|\,dy\leq C.

Note that if we use (
3.130
), the support properties of the
Q_{\nu}^{\theta_{0}}
operators and the finite overlap of the balls
\{B(x_{j},2\delta)\}
we obtain
for our fixed
\ell_{0}=1,\dots,K

(3.131)

\begin{split}\bigl(\sum_{j,\nu}\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}h\|_{L^{q}(M)}^{q}\bigr)^{1/q}\lesssim\|h\|_{L^{q}(M)},\,\,2\leq q\leq\infty\\
\bigl\|\sum_{j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j^{\prime},\ell_{0},\nu^{\prime}})^{*}H(\nu^{\prime},j^{\prime},\,\cdot\,)\|_{L^{p}(M)}\lesssim\|H\|_{\ell^{p}_{\nu^{\prime}}L^{p}(M)},\,\,1\leq p\leq 2.\end{split}

In addition to this inequality and (
3.103
), we shall also require the following commutator bounds

(3.132)

\bigl\|(A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}-A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda})H\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}(M\times[0,T])}\leq C_{q}\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{0}}\|H\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}(B(x_{j},2\delta)\times{\mathbb{R}})},

assuming that
\delta
, as well as
\delta_{1}
in (
3.4
) are fixed small enough.

To see this, if we use the auxiliary operator
\tilde{A}_{j,\ell_{0}}
and Young’s inequality as in the previous section, and apply Bernstein inequality in time, it suffices to show

(3.133)

\bigl\|(A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}-A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda})H\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{2}_{x}(M\times[0,T])}\leq C_{q}\lambda^{-1+2\varepsilon_{0}}\|H\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}(B(x_{j},2\delta)\times{\mathbb{R}})},

since
(n-1)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{q})+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{p}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{2}
for
(p,q)
satisfying (
3.122
).

This follows from the
proof of (2.59) in
[
32
]
since, by (
3.18
),
A_{j,\ell_{0}}f
vanishes outside

B(x_{j},2\delta)
and the two operators in (
3.132
) vanish when acting on functions vanishing on

B(x_{j},2\delta)
. This
allows one to prove (
3.133
),
exactly as in
[
33
]
, by just working in a coordinate chart
(
B(x_{j},2\delta)
here) and, to obtain the inequality
using (
3.127
) and Egorov’s theorem related to the properties of the half wave operator
e^{itP}
in this local coordinate.

Next, as in
[
5
]
and
[
32
]
, if
H=S_{\lambda}f
, we note that we can write for
\theta_{0}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}
as in (
3.123
)

(3.134)

\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}H\bigr)^{2}=\sum_{\nu,\nu^{\prime}}\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}H\bigr)\cdot\bigl(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu^{\prime}}H\bigr)+O(\lambda^{-N}\|H\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t,x}(B(x_{j},2\delta)\times{\mathbb{R}})}),\,\,\forall\,N.

Recall that the
\nu=\theta_{0}\cdot{\mathbb{Z}}^{2(n-2)+1}
index a
\lambda^{-\varepsilon_{0}}
-separated lattice in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2(n-2)+1}
.
If we repeat the Whitney decomposition and the arguments in (
3.62
)-(
3.69
) as in the previous section. We can write

(3.135)

(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}H)^{2}=\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)+\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)

when
n-1\geq 4
. And when
n-1=3
, we further decompose
\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}(H)
and write

(3.136)

(\tilde{\sigma}_{\lambda}H)^{4}\lesssim 2{\overline{\Upsilon}_{j,\ell_{0}}^{\text{diag}}}(H)+2{\overline{\Upsilon}_{j,\ell_{0}}^{\text{far}}}(H)+2(\Upsilon^{\text{far}}(H))^{2}.

Here the operators
\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}},\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}},{\overline{\Upsilon}_{j,\ell_{0}}^{\text{diag}}}
and
{\overline{\Upsilon}_{j,\ell_{0}}^{\text{far}}}
are defined exactly in the same manner as in (
3.63
), (
3.64
) and (
3.68
), except that they now act on functions that also depend on the time variable.
And we are treating the case
n-1=3
separately as
\frac{2(n-1)}{n-3}=6
when
n-1=3
, which requires a slight modification when we use bilinear ideas from
[
47
]
. The remaining case
n-1=2
is analogous to
n-1=3
, we shall briefly outline the necessary arguments in the end of this section.

We shall need the the following variant of Lemma 3.1 in
[
32
]

Lemma 3.7
.

Let
\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-\varepsilon_{0}}
with
\lambda\gg 1
.
If
n-1\geq 4
,
q_{e}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-3}
and
Q^{\theta_{0}}_{\nu}=Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}
as in (
3.126
),

(3.137)

\displaystyle\int

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{j}\int|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}dx\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt

\displaystyle\leq\,\int\big(\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}H\bigr\|^{q_{e}}_{L^{q_{e}}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt+O(\lambda^{1-}\|H\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t,x}}).

Additionally, for
n-1=3
we have

(3.138)

\displaystyle\int

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{j}\int|\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{\frac{q_{e}}{4}}dx\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt

\displaystyle\leq\,\int\big(\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}H\bigr\|^{q_{e}}_{L^{q_{e}}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt+O(\lambda^{1-}\|H\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t,x}}).

In the above and what follows
O(\lambda^{\mu-})
denotes
O(\lambda^{\mu-\varepsilon})
for some
\varepsilon>0
. As we shall see later in the proof, unlike Lemma
3.2
in the previous section, we can not fix
j,\ell_{0}
here. It is crucial that the
\ell^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}_{j}
norm is taken inside the
dt
integral. As in
[
32
]
, since the
Q_{\nu}^{\theta}
operators are time independent,
the main step in the proof of (
3.137
) is to show that
for arbitrary
h_{\nu},h_{\tilde{\nu}}
, which may depend on
\nu
and
\tilde{\nu}
, we have

(3.139)

\displaystyle\bigl\|\sum_{(\nu,\tilde{\nu})\in\Xi_{\theta_{0}}}Q_{\nu,j,\ell_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}h_{\nu}\cdot Q_{\tilde{\nu},j,\ell_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}h_{\tilde{\nu}}\bigr\|_{L^{q_{e}/2}_{x}}\leq C

\displaystyle\Bigl(\sum_{(\nu,\tilde{\nu})\in\Xi_{\theta_{0}}}\|Q_{\nu,j,\ell_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}h_{\nu}\cdot Q_{\tilde{\nu},j,\ell_{0}}^{\theta_{0}}h_{\tilde{\nu}}\|_{L^{q_{e}/2}_{x}}^{q_{e}/2}\,\Bigr)^{2/q_{e}}

\displaystyle+O\big(\lambda^{-N}\sum_{(\nu,\tilde{\nu})\in\Xi_{\theta_{0}}}\|h_{\nu}\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\|h_{\tilde{\nu}}\|_{L^{1}_{x}}\big),\,\,\,\forall N.

The constant
C
here is independent of
j,\ell_{0}
. This result is analogous to (3.20) in
[
32
]
and follows from the same proof provided there. Similarly, the proof of (
3.138
) follows from a variant of (
3.138
) involving the product of four
Q_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}^{\theta_{0}}
operators.

If we fix
\delta
as well as

\delta_{1},\delta_{2}
in (
3.4
) small enough, then we can use Lee’s
[
38
]
bilinear oscillatory integral theorem
and repeat the proof of Lemma 3.2 in
[
5
]
as well as the arguments in (3.38)-(3.42) of
[
32
]
for the case
n-1=3

to obtain the following.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H),\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)
be as above with
\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-\varepsilon_{0}}
and
q=\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n}
. Then for all
\varepsilon>0
there is a
C_{\varepsilon}=C(\varepsilon,M)
so that

(3.140)

\int_{M}|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{q/2}\,dxdt\leq C_{\varepsilon}\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\,\bigl(\lambda^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigr)^{\frac{n-1}{2}(q-\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1})}\|H\|^{q}_{L^{2}_{t,x}(B(x_{j},2\delta)\times{\mathbb{R}})}.

Similarly, for
n-1=3
and
q=\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n}
,

(3.141)

\int_{M}|\overline{\Upsilon}^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{\frac{q}{4}}\,dxdt\leq C_{\varepsilon}\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\,\bigl(\lambda^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigr)^{\frac{n-1}{2}(q-\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1})}\|H\|^{q}_{L^{2}_{t,x}(B(x_{j},2\delta)\times{\mathbb{R}})}.

We now have collected the main ingredients that we need to prove the critical low height estimates.

Proof of (
3.110
) on the set
A_{-}
.

Let us assume that
n-1\geq 4
and thus it suffices to consider
p=2,q=q_{e}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-3}
. A main step in the proof of the
A_{-}
estimates then is to obtain the analog of (2.45) in
[
32
]
.
We shall do so largely by repeating its proof, which we do so for the sake of completeness in order to note the small changes needed
to take into account that, unlike (2.45) in
[
32
]
, (
3.110
) here is a vector valued inequality. As noted before, we have taken
this framework to help us exploit our assumption of bounded geometry, and, in particular, the fact that the doubles of the balls
in our covering of
M
,
\{B(x_{j},2\delta)\}
,
have uniformly finite overlap.

We first note that if
q=\tfrac{2(n+2)}{n}<q_{e}
, then by (
3.113
) and (
3.135
) for our fixed
j,\ell_{0}
we have for
H=S_{\lambda}f

\displaystyle\bigl|\bigl({\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}(H)(x,t,j)\bigr)^{2}\bigr|^{q_{e}/2}=\bigl|\bigl(A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x,t,j)\bigr)^{2}\bigr|^{q_{e}/2}

\displaystyle=|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x,t)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x,t)|^{\frac{q_{e}-q}{2}}\bigl|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)(x,t)+\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)(x,t)\bigr|^{q/2}

\displaystyle\leq|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x,t)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x,t)|^{\frac{q_{e}-q}{2}}2^{q/2}\bigl(|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)(x,t)|^{q/2}+|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)(x,t)|^{q/2}\bigr).

Thus if
A_{-}
is as in (
3.112
),

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}H\|_{L^{2}_{t}L^{q_{e}}_{x}(A_{-})}^{2}=\int\Big(\int\sum_{j}{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,t,j)\,\left|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)|(x,t)\right|^{q_{e}/2}dx\Big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt

\displaystyle\lesssim\int\Big(\sum_{j}\int\bigl[{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,t,j)\,|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x)|^{\frac{q_{e}-q}{2}}\bigr]\,|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)(x)|^{q/2}\Big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt

\displaystyle+\int\Big(\sum_{j}\int\bigl[{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,t,j)\,|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x)|^{\frac{q_{e}-q}{2}}\bigr]\,|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)(x)|^{q/2}dx\Big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt

\displaystyle=C(I+II).

To estimate
II
,
first note that by Hölder’s inequality

(3.142)

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle\lesssim\|{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,t,j)A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(A_{-})}^{\frac{2(q_{e}-q)}{q_{e}}}\cdot\big(\int\big(\sum_{j}\int|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{\frac{q}{2}}dx\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim T^{1-\frac{2}{q_{e}}}\|{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,t,j)A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x,t)\|_{L^{\infty}(A_{-})}^{\frac{2(q_{e}-q)}{q_{e}}}\cdot\big(\sum_{j}\int\int|\Upsilon^{\text{far}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{\frac{q}{2}}dxdt\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}},

Recall that by (
3.112
) and (
3.113
),

|{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,t,j)A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x,t)|\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{4}+\varepsilon_{1}}.

Thus by (
3.140
)

(3.143)

\displaystyle II\leq T^{1-\frac{2}{q_{e}}}

\displaystyle\lambda^{(\frac{n-1}{4}+\varepsilon_{1})(\frac{2(q_{e}-q)}{q_{e}})}

\displaystyle\cdot\Big(\lambda^{1+\varepsilon}\,\bigl(\lambda^{1-\varepsilon_{0}}\bigr)^{\frac{n-1}{2}(q-\frac{2(n+1)}{n-1})}\Big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}(\sum_{j}\|H\|^{q}_{L^{2}_{t,x}(B(x_{j},2\delta)\times{\mathbb{R}})})^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}.

If we take
\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}
and
\varepsilon
to be small enough, e.g.,
\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{1}=\frac{1}{100}
and
\varepsilon_{0}=\frac{1}{2n+2}
, it is straightforward to check that

(3.144)

\displaystyle II\lesssim\lambda^{1-}(\sum_{j}\|H\|^{q}_{L^{2}_{t,x}(B(x_{j},2\delta)\times{\mathbb{R}})})^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}\lesssim\lambda^{1-}\|H\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}^{\frac{2q}{q_{e}}}=O(\lambda^{1-}\|H\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}^{2}).

Here we also used the fact that

\|H\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}^{2}
dominates

\|H\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}^{\frac{2q}{q_{e}}}
since
q_{e}>q
and

\|H\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\approx T
since
H=S_{\lambda}f
,
\|f\|_{2}=1

and
e^{-it\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}
is a unitary operator on
L^{2}_{x}
.

To control
I
, as in
[
32
]
, we
use Hölder’s inequality followed by Young’s
inequality along with (
3.137
) to get

(3.145)

\displaystyle I=

\displaystyle\int\Big(\sum_{j}\int\bigl[{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}(x,t,j)\,|A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x)\cdot A_{j,\ell_{0}}(\sigma_{\lambda}H)(x)|^{\frac{q_{e}-q}{2}}\bigr]\,|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)(x)|^{q/2}\Big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int\Big(\|{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}\cdot{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}H\cdot{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}H\|_{\ell_{j}^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}L_{x}^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}}^{\frac{q_{e}-q}{2}}\,\big(\sum_{j}\int|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}dx\big)^{\frac{q}{q_{e}}}\,\Big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|{\bf 1}_{A_{-}}{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}H\cdot{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}H\|_{L^{1}_{t}\ell_{j}^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}L_{x}^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}}^{\frac{q_{e}-q}{q_{e}}}\,\big(\int\big(\sum_{j}\int|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}dx\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt\big)^{\frac{q}{q_{e}}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{q_{e}-q}{q_{e}}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}H\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t}\ell_{j}^{q_{e}}L_{x}^{q_{e}}(A_{-})}+\tfrac{q}{q_{e}}\big(\int\big(\sum_{j}\int|\Upsilon^{\text{diag}}_{j,\ell_{0}}(H)|^{\frac{q_{e}}{2}}dx\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\tfrac{q_{e}-q}{q_{e}}\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}H\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t}\ell_{j}^{q_{e}}L_{x}^{q_{e}}(A_{-})}+C(\,\int\big(\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}H\bigr\|^{q_{e}}_{L^{q_{e}}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q_{e}}}dt)

\displaystyle+O(\lambda^{1-}\|H\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t,x}}).

If we combine the above two estimate, we have the follow which is the analog of proposition 2.3 in
[
32
]
.

Proposition 3.9
.

Fix a complete
n-1\geq 2
dimensional Riemannian manifold
(M,g)
of bounded geometry and assume that (
3.111
) is valid.
If
H=S_{\lambda}f
is as in (
3.96
),
(p,q)
satisfies (
3.122
) and
\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1}
in the definition of
A_{-}
and
\theta_{0}
are small enough, we have

(3.146)

\|{\mathcal{A}}\sigma_{\lambda}H\|_{L^{p}_{t}L^{q}_{x}\ell_{j}^{q}(A_{-})}\lesssim\big(\,\int\big(\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}H\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}-}.

The case
n-1\geq 4
in (
3.146
) directly follows from the above estimates for
I
and
II
.
One can similarly use (
3.138
) and (
3.141
) and
modify the arguments in
[
32
]

to handle the case when
n-1=3
.

The arguments for
n-1=2
and general
(p,q)
in (
3.122
) is similar to the case
n-1=3
. Recall that when
n-1=3
,
q_{e}=\frac{2(n-1)}{n-3}=6\in[2^{2},2^{3}]
. As a result, an additional round of
Whitney decomposition is needed for
(\Upsilon^{\text{diag}})^{2}
in order to get the desired estimate (
3.138
) in Lemma
3.7
. When
n-1=2
,
q
can be arbitrary large, if
q\in[2^{k+1},2^{k+2}]
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, then one can repeat the arguments for
n-1=3
in
[
32
]

k
times, the resulting diagonal term will involve a product of
2^{k+1}
terms of involving
A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}H
and will satisfy the analog of (
3.138
) with
q_{e}/4
replaced by
q/2^{k}
. Each iteration of
Whitney decomposition also generates off-diagonal terms,
which can be treated using bilinear oscillatory integral estimates. However, as
q\rightarrow\infty
, unlike (
3.143
), we need to take
\varepsilon_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{1}
to be small enough depending on
q
, instead of some fixed small constant.

Thus to prove (
3.110
), it remains to control the first term on the right side of (
3.146
).
By (
3.132
) along with the fact that
\ell^{2}\subset\ell^{q}
if
q\geq 2
, we have

(3.147)

\displaystyle\big(\,\int

\displaystyle\big(\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}H\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(\,\int\big(\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}H\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\big(\,\int\big(\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|(A_{j,\ell_{0}}\sigma_{\lambda}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}-A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu})\sigma_{\lambda}H\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\big(\int\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}H\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}+2\varepsilon_{0}}\big(\sum_{\nu}\|H\,\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{t,x}}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since the number of choices of
\nu
is
O(\lambda^{(2n-3)\varepsilon_{0}})
and
H
is independent of
\nu
, the second term in the right is dominated by
\lambda^{(n-\frac{3}{2})\varepsilon_{0}}\|\,H\,\|_{L^{2}_{t,x}}
. Thus if we choose
\varepsilon_{0}<\tfrac{1}{2n+1}
, the second term on the right side of (
3.147
) is

O(\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}-})
.

Next recall that
H=S_{\lambda}f
and
\|f\|_{2}=1
, if we use (
3.20
), (
3.131
), followed by (
3.103
), we can control the term in the right as follows

(3.148)

\displaystyle\big(\int

\displaystyle\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|A_{j,\ell_{0}}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}(B(x_{j},2\delta))}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\int\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\sigma_{\lambda}S_{\lambda}f\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\int\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}S_{\lambda}f\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\big(\int\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}(I-\sigma_{\lambda})S_{\lambda}f\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\int\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}S_{\lambda}f\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\big(\int\bigl\|(I-\sigma_{\lambda})S_{\lambda}f\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\big(\int\sum_{j,\nu}\bigl\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}S_{\lambda}f\bigr\|^{q}_{L^{q}_{x}}\big)^{\frac{2}{q}}dt\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\lambda^{\frac{1}{p}}T^{\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{2}}.

If we combine (
3.146
) and the preceding two inequalities we conclude that we would obtain (
3.110
) and consequently
finish the proof of the estimates in Theorem
1.3
if, for
(p,q)
as in (
3.122
) and
T
as in (
3.96
), we could show that

(3.149)

Uf(t,x,j,\nu)=(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}S_{\lambda}f)(x,t),

satisfies

(3.150)

\|Uf\|_{L^{p}_{t}\ell_{j}^{q}\ell_{\nu}^{q}L^{q}_{x}}\lesssim\lambda^{\frac{1}{q}}\|f\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

We shall require the following lemma

Lemma 3.10
.

Fix
t,j,\ell_{0},\nu
, let
K_{t,\lambda}
denote the operator

\eta(t/T)Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\beta(P/\lambda)e^{-it\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}.

Then if
(M,g)
is a complete manifold of bounded geometry all of whose sectional curvatures are nonpositive and
T=c_{0}\log\lambda
is fixed with
c_{0}=c_{0}(M)>0
sufficiently small, we have for
\lambda\gg 1

(3.151)

\|K_{t,\lambda}K^{*}_{s,\lambda}\|_{L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}.

We shall postpone the proof of this lemma until the end of this section and first see
how we can use it to prove (
3.150
).
By applying the abstract theorem of Keel-Tao
[
37
]
and a simple rescaling argument,
we
would have (
3.150
) if

(3.152)

\|Uf(t,\cdot)\|_{\ell^{2}_{j}\ell_{\nu}^{2}L^{2}_{x}}\leq C\|f\|_{L^{2}_{x}},

and

(3.153)

\|U(t)U^{*}(s)G\|_{\ell^{\infty}_{j}\ell_{\nu}^{\infty}L^{\infty}_{x}}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\,|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\|G\|_{\ell_{j}^{1}\ell_{\nu}^{1}L^{1}_{x}},

with

(3.154)

\displaystyle\big(U(t)U^{*}(s)G\big)(x,j,\nu)=

\displaystyle=\eta(t/T)\sum_{j^{\prime},\nu^{\prime}}\eta(s/T)\Bigl[\bigr(Q_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}^{\theta_{0}}e^{-i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j^{\prime},\ell_{0},\nu^{\prime}})^{*}\bigr)G(\,\cdot,\,j^{\prime},\,\nu^{\prime})\Bigr](x).,

It is not hard to check that (
3.152
) follows from (
3.131
) with
p=2
and the fact that
e^{-it\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}
is unitary, and (
3.153
) follows from the estimate (
3.151
).
∎

3.3. Kernel estimates

Let us start out by proving the bounds in Lemmas
3.1
and
3.5
that were used to prove the spectral projection
estimates in Theorem
1.5
.

Proof of Lemma
3.1
.

We first note that since
P
is nonnegative, if we replace
e^{-itP}
in (
3.35
) with
e^{itP}
, then, by (
1.4
),
the resulting operator maps
L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)
with norm
O(\lambda^{-N})\,\forall\,N
. Thus, by Euler’s formula, if

(3.155)

\tilde{G}_{\lambda}(x,y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(1-a(t))\,T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\,\bigl(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{g}}\bigr)(x,y)\,dt,

it suffices to show that, under the assumptions of Lemma
3.1
, we have

(3.156)

\tilde{G}_{\lambda}(x,y)=O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\exp(C_{M}T)),

assuming that
T=c_{0}\log\lambda
, with
c_{0}=c_{0}(M)>0
sufficiently small.

To prove this, we can use the arguments of Bérard
[
3
]
. Indeed, if we use the covering map coming from
the exponential map
\kappa=\exp_{x}:T_{x}M\simeq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to M
at
x
, then
\kappa
is a covering map and

({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})

\kappa^{*}g=\tilde{g}
, is the universal cover. Like
(M,g)
, all of the sectional curvatures of

({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
are nonpositive. As in (
2.102
) above, let
\Gamma
be the associated
deck transformations and choose a Dirichlet domain
D
associated with the origin, which is in the lift of
x
.
If
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}
are the lifts to
D
of
x,y\in M
, we have the formula

(3.157)

\bigl(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{g}}\bigr)(x,y)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}\bigl(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}\bigr)(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y})).

As a result,

(3.158)

\tilde{G}_{\lambda}(x,y)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(1-a(t))\,T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\,\bigl(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}\bigr)(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\,dt.

To use this formula, we first note that, by (
3.4
),

\hat{\Psi}(s)=0
if
|s|>2
, which means that the integrands in (
3.158
) vanishes for
|t|>2T
. Also, by finite propagation
speed for the wave operator,

\bigl(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}\bigr)(\tilde{x},\tilde{z})=0\,\,\text{if }\,\,d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{z})>|t|,

and so each of the summands in (
3.158
)

(3.159)

K_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(1-a(t))\,T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\,\bigl(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}\bigr)(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\,dt=0,\\
\text{if }\,\,d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))>2T=2c_{0}\log\lambda.

Furthermore, if
c_{0}>0
here is small enough then since
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
is of bounded geometry and all of its
sectional curvatures are nonpositive, as in
[
3
]
,
[
42
, §3.6]
, one can use the
Hadamard parametrix
and stationary phase arguments to see that for
T
as above one has the uniform bounds

(3.160)

K_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}).

As a result, we would obtain the bound (
3.156
) if we could verify that there are
O(\exp(C_{M}T))
nonzero summands
in (
3.158
) for
T
as above. To do this, we let
r=r_{\rm{Inj}}(M)/4
. Then if
B_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{z},r)
is the geodesic ball
in
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
with center
\tilde{z}
and radius
r
, we must have

(3.161)

B_{\tilde{g}}(\alpha(\tilde{y}),r)\cap B_{\tilde{g}}(\alpha^{\prime}(\tilde{y}),r)=\emptyset\,\,\text{if }\,\,\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime},\,\,\text{and }\,\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}\in\Gamma.

Note that, by the above, in order for
K_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
to be nonzero
we must also have that
B_{\tilde{g}}(\alpha(\tilde{y}),r)\subset B_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},2T+r)
.
Additionally, the volume of
B_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{z},r)
must be
O(1)
due to the fact that
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
is
of bounded geometry. Similarly, since the sectional curvatures of
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
must be bounded below,
by standard volume comparison theorems (see e.g.,
[
19
]
) the volume of

B_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},2T+r)
must be
O(\exp(C_{M}T))
, assuming, as we may that
T>r
. These two crude
volume estimates along with (
3.161
) yield the above claim about the number of nonzero summands
in (
3.159
), which finishes the proof.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

In view of the first estimate in (
3.55
) for
q=\infty
, we can use Euler’s formula as above to see that
we would have (
3.86
) if we could show that for
T=c_{0}\log\lambda
with
c_{0}>0
sufficiently small we have
for
\lambda\gg 1

(3.162)

Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\tilde{G}_{\lambda,N}(x,y)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(1-a(t))\,T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\,\beta(|t|/N)\,Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\bigl(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{g}}\bigr)(x,y)\,dt\\
=O(T^{-1}\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}N^{1-\frac{n-1}{2}}),

assuming that the sectional curvatures of
(M,g)
are nonpositive.

We can use (
3.157
) to write

(3.163)

Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\tilde{G}_{\lambda,N}(x,y)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}K^{j,\ell_{0},\nu}_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y}),\,\,\text{where }\\
K^{j,\ell_{0},\nu}_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}(1-a(t))T^{-1}\hat{\Psi}(t/T)\beta(|t|/N)Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\bigl(\cos t\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}\bigr)(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\,dt,

abusing notation a bit here by letting
Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}
here denote the lift of the operator on
(M,g)

to
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
via the covering map.

Since the integrand in (
3.163
) vanishes when
|t|\notin(N/2,2N)
one can use the Hadamard parametrix along
with (
3.55
) to see that, by the arguments in
[
6
]
,

(3.164)

K^{j,\ell_{0},\nu}_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\begin{cases}O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}N^{-\frac{n-1}{2}})\,\,\,\text{if }\,d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\in[N/4,4N]\\
O(\lambda^{-m})\,\,\forall\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}\,\,\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

if
T=c_{0}\log\lambda
with
c_{0}>0
sufficiently small.

This, by itself will not yield (
3.86
). For this, let
\tilde{\gamma}=\tilde{\gamma}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

be the geodesic through the origin of the lift of the geodesic
\gamma_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\subset M
associated
with
Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}
. Then the arguments in
[
6
]
also yield that if

T=c_{0}\log\lambda
with
c_{0}>0
small enough one has

(3.165)

K^{j,\ell_{0},\nu}_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=O(\lambda^{-m})\,\,\forall\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}\,\,\text{if }\,d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{\gamma},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\geq C_{0},

for some fixed
C_{0}=C_{0}(M)
. Since we can also use the volume counting arguments in
[
6
]
to see that
that number of
\alpha\in\Gamma
for which
d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\in[N/4,4N]

and
d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{\gamma},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\leq C_{0}
is
O(N)
, we obtain
(
3.86
) from (
3.163
), (
3.164
) and (
3.165
).

If we assume that the sectional curvatures of
(M,g)
, and hence
({\mathbb{R}}^{n},\tilde{g})
, are pinched below zero as in
(
3.87
), then we have much more favorable dispersive estimates for the main term in the Hadamard parametrix,
as noted in
[
4
]
and
[
33
]
. This leads to the improvement of the first part of (
3.164
) under this
curvature assumption:

(3.166)

K^{j,\ell_{0},\nu}_{\alpha}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=O_{m}(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}N^{-m})\,\,\forall\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}.

By using this along with the above arguments, we obtain the other estimate, (
3.87
), in
Lemma
3.5
.
∎

Now we shall prove the bounds that were used for the Strichartz estimates.

Proof of Lemma
3.6
.

To prove (
3.114
), we shall mostly follow the proof of Proposition 4.1 in
[
5
]
as well as the ideas in the proof of Lemma
3.1
above. Note that for fixed
t
and
s
,

\beta^{2}(P/\lambda)e^{-i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}=\beta^{2}(P/\lambda)e^{i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}P^{2}}
is the Fourier multiplier operator
on
M
with

(3.167)

m(\lambda,t-s;\tau)=\beta^{2}(|\tau|/\lambda)e^{i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}\tau^{2}}.

We have extended
m
to be an even function of
\tau
so that we can write

(3.168)

\beta^{2}(P/\lambda)e^{-i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)\,\cos r\sqrt{-\Delta_{g}}\,dr,

where

(3.169)

\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-i\tau r}\beta^{2}(|\tau|/\lambda)\,e^{i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}\tau^{2}}\,d\tau.

We note that, by a simple integration by parts argument,

(3.170)

\partial^{k}_{r}\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)=O(\lambda^{-N}(1+|r|)^{-N})\,\forall\,N,\\
\text{if }\,|t-s|\leq 2^{j},\,\,\text{and }\,\,|r|\geq C_{0}2^{j},\,\,j=0,1,2,\dots,

with
C_{0}
fixed large enough. Since
\beta(|\tau|/\lambda)=0
if
|\tau|\notin[\lambda/4,2\lambda]
one may take

C_{0}=100
, as we shall do.

To use this fix an even function
a\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}})
satisfying

a(r)=1,\,\,|r|\leq 100\quad\text{and }\,\,a(r)=0\,\,\text{if }\,\,|r|\geq 200.

Then if
we let

(3.171)

\tilde{S}_{\lambda,j}(t,s)(P)=(2\pi)^{-1}\int a(2^{-j}r)\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s,r)\cos rP\,dr

we have the symbol
F_{\lambda,j}(\tau)
of the multiplier operator

F_{\lambda,j}(P)=\tilde{S}_{\lambda,j}(t,s)(P)-\beta^{2}(P/\lambda)e^{i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}P^{2}}

is
O(\lambda^{-N_{1}}(1+\tau)^{-N_{2}})\,\,\,\forall N_{1},N_{2}
if
|t-s|\leq 2^{j}
. Thus by (
1.4
) we have

\|F_{\lambda,j}(P)\|_{L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}\lesssim 1,\,\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,|t-s|\leq 2^{j}.

Consequently, if we let
\tilde{S}_{\lambda,j}(x,t;y,s)
denote
the kernel of the multiplier operator
\tilde{S}_{\lambda,j}(t,s)(P)
,
we would have (
3.114
) if we could show that

(3.172)

|\tilde{S}_{\lambda,j}(x,t;y,s)|\leq\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\exp(C2^{j}),\quad\text{if }\,|t-s|\leq 2^{j}\\
\text{with }\,\,j=0,1,2,\dots\,\,\text{and }\,\,\,2^{j}\leq c_{0}\log\lambda

with
c_{0}=c_{0}(M)
fixed small enough.

To prove (
3.172
), as in the proof of Lemma
3.1
, we shall use the
Hadamard parametrix and the
Cartan-Hadamard theorem to lift the
calculations that will be needed up to the
universal cover
({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1},\tilde{g})

of
(M,g)
. Let
\Gamma
be the associated
deck transformations and choose a Dirichlet domain
D
associated with the origin.
If
\tilde{x},\tilde{y}
are the lifts to
D
of
x,y\in M
,
by (
2.102
) if we set

(3.173)

K_{\lambda,j}(\tilde{x},t;\tilde{y},s)=(2\pi)^{-1}\int a(2^{-j}r)\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)\,\bigl(\cos r\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}\bigr)(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\,dr,

we have the formula

(3.174)

\tilde{S}_{\lambda,j}(x,t;y,s)=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}K_{\lambda,j}(\tilde{x},t;\alpha(\tilde{y}),s).

Also, by Huygen’s principle and the support properties
of
a
, we have that

(3.175)

K_{\lambda,j}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=0\,\,\text{if }\,\,d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\leq C_{1}2^{j}

for a uniform constant
C_{1}
. Based on this, if we argue as in the proof of Lemma
3.1
using (
3.161
) along with simple volume estimates related to the bounded geometry assumption, it is not hard to show that
the number
of non-zero summands on the right side of (
3.174
) is
O(\exp(C2^{j}))
. As a result, we would obtain (
3.172
) if we could show that

(3.176)

|K_{\lambda,j}(\tilde{x},t;\tilde{y},s)|\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\tfrac{n-1}{2}},\quad\text{if }\,|t-s|\leq 2^{j}\,\,\\
\text{with}\,\,\,j=0,1,2,\dots,\,2^{j}\leq c_{0}\log\lambda.

As in the previous section, to prove (
3.176
), we can use the Hadamard
parametrix for
\partial_{r}^{2}-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}

since
({\mathbb{R}}^{n-1},\tilde{g})
is a Riemannian
manifold without conjugate points, i.e., its
injectivity radius is infinite. More explicitly for

\tilde{x}\in D
,
\tilde{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}

and
|r|>0

(3.177)

\bigl(\cos r\sqrt{-\Delta}_{\tilde{g}}\bigr)(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=\sum_{\nu=0}^{N}w_{\nu}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})W_{\nu}(r,\tilde{x},\tilde{y})+R_{N}(r,\tilde{x},\tilde{y})

where
w_{\nu},W_{\nu}
and
R_{N}
satisfies (
2.75
)-(
2.79
).

By (
3.177
), it suffices
to see that if we replace
(\cos r\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}})(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
in (
3.173
) by each
of the terms in the right side of (
3.177
) then each such expression will satisfy the bounds
in (
3.176
).

Let us start with the contribution of the main term in the Hadamard parametrix which is the

\nu=0
term in (
3.177
). In view of (
2.75
) and (
2.78
) it would give rise to these
bounds if

(3.178)

(2\pi)^{-n}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}e^{id_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\xi_{1}}\cos(r|\xi|)\,a(2^{-j}r)\,\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)\,drd\xi\\
=O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}})\quad\text{when }\,\,\,|t-s|\leq 2^{j}.

However, by (
3.167
) and (
3.170
) and the support properties of
a
,

(3.179)

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{-1}

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}e^{id_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\xi_{1}}\cos(r|\xi|)\,a(2^{-j}r)\,\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)\,drd\xi

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}e^{id_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\xi_{1}}\cos(r|\xi|)\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)\,drd\xi+O(\lambda^{-N})

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}e^{id_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\xi_{1}}\beta^{2}(|\xi|/\lambda)e^{i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}|\xi|^{2}}\,d\xi+O(\lambda^{-N}).

A simple stationary phase argument shows that the last integral is
O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}})
, and so
we conclude that the main term in the Hadamard parametrix leads to the desired bounds.

Similarly, one can use stationary phase to show that if
|t-s|\leq 2^{j}

(3.180)

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{-1}\iint

\displaystyle e^{id_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\xi_{1}}e^{\pm ir|\xi|}\alpha_{\nu}(|\xi|)\,a(2^{-j}r)\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)\,drd\xi

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{n-1}}e^{id_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\xi_{1}}\beta^{2}(|\xi|/\lambda)e^{i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}|\xi|^{2}}\,\alpha_{\nu}(|\xi|)\,d\xi+O(\lambda^{-N})

\displaystyle=O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}-\nu}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}).

Note that by (
3.175
)
we may assume that
d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\leq Cc_{0}\log\lambda
. So by (
3.177
) and (
2.79
), if we choose
c_{0}
small enough, the contributions from the higher order terms would
be

O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}})
.

We also need to see that the remainder term in (
3.177
) leads to the bounds

(3.181)

\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}a(2^{-j}r)\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)R(r,\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\,dr\\
=\int^{\infty}_{-\infty}\beta^{2}(|\tau|/\lambda)e^{i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}\tau^{2}}\,\bigl[a(2^{-j}\,\cdot\,)R(\,\cdot\,,\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\bigr]\,\widehat{}\,\,(\tau)\,d\tau=O(\lambda^{-N}),\,\,\forall N.

Since we are assuming that
d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\leq Cc_{0}\log\lambda
,
by (
2.77
) and support properties of
\alpha
, the last factor in the integral in the right, which is the Fourier transform of

r\to a(r)R(r,\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
, is
O(|\tau|^{-N}\exp(CNc_{0}\log\lambda))
. So, by the support properties of
\beta
, the last integral
in (
3.181
) is
O(\lambda^{-N})
if we fix
c_{0}
small enough.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.10
.

If we use the second part of (
3.130
), it suffices to show

(3.182)

\|\eta(t/T)\eta(s/T)Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\beta^{2}(P/\lambda)e^{-i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}\Delta_{g}}\|_{L^{1}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}.

Recall that by the first part of (
3.130
), we have
\|Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\|_{L^{\infty}(M)\to L^{\infty}(M)}\leq C
. Thus if we repeat the arguments in the proof of Lemma
3.6
above, it suffices to show

(3.183)

|\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}K_{\lambda,j}(\tilde{x},t;\alpha(\tilde{y}),s))|\leq C\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\tfrac{n-1}{2}},\,\,\quad\text{if }\,|t-s|\leq 2^{j}\\
\text{with}\,\,\,j=0,1,2,\dots,\,2^{j}\leq c_{0}\log\lambda.

where

(3.184)

K_{\lambda,j}(\tilde{x},t;\tilde{y},s)=(2\pi)^{-1}\int a(2^{-j}r)\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)\,\big(Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\circ\,\cos r\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}\big)(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\,dr,

and

(3.185)

K_{\lambda,j}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})=0\,\,\text{if }\,\,d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\geq C_{1}2^{j}

for a uniform constant
C_{1}
. As in (
3.174
),
the number
of non-zero summands on the right side of (
3.183
) is
O(\exp(C2^{j}))
.

If we repeat the arguments in (
3.178
)-(
3.181
), it suffices to replace
\cos r\sqrt{-\Delta_{\tilde{g}}}
by the main term in the Hadamard parametrix as the higher order terms and remainder term will contribute errors of

O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}})
as long as we choose
c_{0}
small enough as above. Thus the proof of Lemma
3.10
would be complete if we can show that

(3.186)

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{-2n-1}

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\iiint e^{i((\tilde{x}-\tilde{z})\cdot\eta+d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{z},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\xi_{1})}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}(\tilde{x},\tilde{z},\eta)

\displaystyle\cdot\cos(r|\xi|)\,a(2^{-j}r)\,\hat{m}(\lambda,t-s;r)\,drd\eta d\tilde{y}d\xi

\displaystyle=O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}})\quad\text{when }\,\,\,|t-s|\leq 2^{j}.

As in (
3.179
),
by (
3.167
) and (
3.170
) and the support properties of
a
, each term in the summand of (
3.186
) can be simplified as

\displaystyle(2\pi)^{-2n-2}\int e^{i((\tilde{x}-\tilde{z})\cdot\eta+d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{z},\alpha(\tilde{y}))}Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}(\tilde{x},\tilde{z},\eta)\beta^{2}(|\xi|/\lambda)e^{i(t-s)\lambda^{-1}|\xi|^{2}}\,d\eta d\tilde{y}d\xi+O(\lambda^{-N}).

Here
Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}(\tilde{x},\tilde{z},\eta)
is the symbol for the operator
Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}
.

By a simple stationary phase argument, the last integral is
O(\lambda^{\frac{n-1}{2}}|t-s|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}})
. On the other hand,
recall that as in (
3.126
)

Q^{\theta}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}=Q^{\theta}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu^{\prime}}\circ Q^{\theta}_{j,\ell_{0},\ell}.
If we let
\tilde{\gamma}=\tilde{\gamma}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

be the geodesic through the origin of the lift of the geodesic
\gamma_{j,\ell_{0},\nu}\subset M
associated
with
Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\nu^{\prime}}
and
\kappa^{\theta_{0}}_{\ell}
as in the definition of
Q^{\theta_{0}}_{j,\ell_{0},\ell}
in (
3.124
), then
one can follow the arguments in the proof of Proposition 4.2 in
[
5
]
to see that the last integral is
O(\lambda^{-m})\,\,\forall\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
unless

(3.187)

d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{\gamma},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\leq C_{0},

and

(3.188)

d_{\tilde{g}}(\tilde{x},\alpha(\tilde{y}))\in\bigl[2|t-s|(\kappa^{\theta_{0}}_{\ell}-C_{0}\lambda^{-\varepsilon_{0}}),\,2|t-s|(\kappa^{\theta_{0}}_{\ell}+C_{0}\lambda^{-\varepsilon_{0}})\big]

for some fixed
C_{0}=C_{0}(M)
and
\theta_{0}=\lambda^{-\varepsilon_{0}}
. By (
3.161
) with simple volume counting arguments, one can see
that number of
\alpha\in\Gamma
for which (
3.187
) and (
3.188
) hold is
O(1)
. This finishes the proof of Lemma
3.10
.
∎

4.
Littlewood-Paley estimates

Lemma 4.1
.

Let

\beta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(1/2,2)
with
\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\beta(s/2^{k})=1
, and define
\beta_{k}(s)=\beta(s/2^{k})
,
\beta_{0}(s)=\sum_{k\leq 0}\beta(s/2^{k})
. If
(M,g)
is a complete manifold of bounded geometry, we have for
2\leq q<\infty

(4.1)

\|u\|_{L^{q}(M)}\lesssim\|Su\|_{L^{q}(M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)},

where
Su=\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}|\beta_{k}(P)u|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
with
P=\sqrt{-\Delta_{g}}
.

Lemma
4.1
is a generalization of Bouclet
[
9
, Theorem 1.3]
to complete manifolds of bounded geometry, and its proof mostly follows from the same arguments there. For the sake of completeness, we provide the detailed proof below.

On compact manifolds, the above estimate holds without the additional term
\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}
. However, on non-compact manifolds, the estimate may fail without this term, since otherwise it would imply the
L^{q}
boundness of the multiplier operator
\beta_{k}(P)
. See
[
1
]
for a discussion in the context of hyperbolic spaces.

By Minkowski’s integral inequality, (
4.1
) implies

(4.2)

\|u\|_{L^{q}(M)}\lesssim\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}\|\beta_{k}(P)u\|^{2}_{L^{q}(M)}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

This combined with Theorem
1.3
and
L^{2}
orthogonality yield
Corollary
1.4
.

To prove Lemma
4.1
, we shall require the following

Lemma 4.2
.

For
k\geq 1
, we can write

\beta_{k}(P)=B_{k}+C_{k}
with

(4.3)

\|\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{k}B_{k}u\|_{L^{q}(M)}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{q}(M)},\,\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,a_{k}=\pm 1\,\,\forall k\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,1<q<\infty.

And for
q\geq 2
,

(4.4)

\|C_{k}u\|_{L^{q}(M)}\lesssim_{N}2^{-Nk}\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

Proof.

We can extend
\beta\in C_{0}^{\infty}(1/2,2)
to an even function by letting
\beta(s)=\beta(|s|)
. For
\delta<r_{\rm{Inj}}(M)/2
, we can fix
\rho\in C_{0}^{\infty}
satisfying
\rho(t)=1
,
|t|\leq\delta/2
and
\rho(t)=0
,
|t|\geq\delta
, and define

(4.5)

\displaystyle\beta_{k}(P)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-1}\int\hat{\beta}_{k}(t)\cos tPdt

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-1}\int\rho(t)\hat{\beta}_{k}(t)\cos tPdt+(2\pi)^{-1}\int(1-\rho(t))\hat{\beta}_{k}(t)\cos tPdt

\displaystyle=B_{k}+C_{k}.

It is not hard to check that the symbol of
C_{k}
is
O((1+|\tau|+2^{k})^{-N})
, thus (
4.4
) follows from Sobolev estimates. To prove (
4.3
), we cover
M
by geodesic balls of radius
\delta
. Using the finite propagation speed property of the wave propagator
\cos tP
and locally finite property of the covering, we can reduce the calculations needed to a fixed geodesic ball. Then, (
4.3
) follows from standard arguments using the Hadamard parametrix for
\cos tP
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

Let us denote
S_{B}=\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}|B_{k}u|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Note that by using a standard argument using Rademacher functions( see e.g.,
[
43
, § 0]
), (
4.3
) implies the following square function estimate

(4.6)

\|S_{B}u\|_{L^{q}(M)}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{q}(M)},\,\,1<q<\infty.

Since
\beta_{k_{1}}(P)\beta_{k_{2}}(P)\equiv 0
if
|k_{1}-k_{2}|\geq 2
, we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\int_{M}

\displaystyle u_{1}\bar{u}_{2}dx\lesssim\|S_{B}u_{1}\|_{L^{q}(M)}\|S_{B}u_{2}\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}(M)}+\|u_{2}\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}(M)}

\displaystyle\times\big(\sum_{\{k_{1},k_{2}\geq 0,|k_{1}-k_{2}|\leq 1\}}\|B_{k_{1}}C_{k_{2}}u_{1}\|_{L^{q}(M)}+\|C_{k_{1}}B_{k_{2}}u_{1}\|_{L^{q}(M)}+\|C_{k_{1}}C_{k_{2}}u_{1}\|_{L^{q}(M)}\big)

By (
4.3
), we have
\|B_{k}\|_{L^{q}(M)\to L^{q}(M)}\lesssim 1
. If we combine this with (
4.6
) and (
4.4
), it is not hard to show that

(4.8)

\int_{M}u_{1}\bar{u}_{2}dx\lesssim\|u_{2}\|_{L^{q^{\prime}}(M)}(\|S_{B}u_{1}\|_{L^{q}(M)}+\|u_{1}\|_{L^{2}(M)}).

This implies

(4.9)

\|u\|_{L^{q}(M)}\lesssim\|S_{B}u\|_{L^{q}(M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

To replace
S_{B}
by
S
, let us define
S_{C}=\left(\sum_{k\geq 0}|C_{k}u|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then

(4.10)

\displaystyle\|S_{B}u\|_{L^{q}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\|Su\|_{L^{q}(M)}+\|S_{C}u\|_{L^{q}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\|Su\|_{L^{q}(M)}+\sum_{k\geq 0}\|C_{k}u\|_{L^{q}(M)}

\displaystyle\leq\|Su\|_{L^{q}(M)}+\|u\|_{L^{2}(M)}.

In the last inequality we used (
4.4
). This finished the proof of Lemma
4.1

∎
